Contragredient Lie algebras in symmetric categories
Iván Angiono, Julia Plavnik, Guillermo Sanmarco

Abstract
. We define contragredient Lie algebras in symmetric categories, generalizing the construction of Lie algebras of the form 𝔤 ⁡ ( A ) \mathfrak{g}(A) for a Cartan matrix A A from the category of vector spaces to an arbitrary symmetric tensor category. The main complication resides in the fact that, in contrast to the classical case, a general symmetric tensor category can admit tori (playing the role of Cartan subalgebras) which are non-abelian and have a sophisticated representation theory. Using this construction, we obtain and describe new examples of Lie algebras in the universal Verlinde category in characteristic p ≥ 5 p\geq 5 . We also show that some previously known examples can be obtained with our construction.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Analogs of Deligne’s theorem in positive characteristic

By classical results of Deligne
[
10
,
11
]
, pre-Tannakian categories (symmetric tensor categories wherein all objects have finite length) of moderate growth over a field
\Bbbk
of characteristic zero (always assumed algebraically closed in this work) are well understood. It turns out that any such category
\mathcal{C}
admits a (unique) symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
to the category of finite-dimensional super vector spaces, and can thus be reconstructed from group scheme theory in
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
. If we regard pre-Tannakian categories of moderate growth as potential habitats to develop commutative algebra or algebraic geometry, Deligne’s results essentially say that, in characteristic zero, we should content ourselves with the categories of ordinary and super vector spaces, plus some (super) group equivariant structure.

It is also classically known
[
18
,
19
]
that if we take
\Bbbk
of characteristic
p>0
, the theory of pre-Tannakian categories of moderate growth over
\Bbbk
becomes richer. However, the subject only started to enjoy a more systematic development after Ostrik’s recent outbreak
[
24
]
, which sparked the deployment of intensive research efforts such as
[
2
,
7
,
8
,
6
,
5
,
4
,
9
,
17
,
13
,
15
,
27
,
28
,
29
]
.

Assume from now on that
\Bbbk
has positive characteristic
p
. Even though still out of reach, a path towards an understanding of pre-Tannakian categories of moderate growth over
\Bbbk
emerges from some of the developments cited above. The main organizational tool behind said path is the notion of incompressible symmetric category. This is a pre-Tannakian category
\mathcal{D}
for which any symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{C}
is an embedding; informally, this means that
\mathcal{D}
can not be constructed from group scheme theory in a smaller symmetric tensor category. Thanks to
[
5
]
, every pre-Tannakian category of moderate growth admits a symmetric functor to some incompressible category of moderate growth. Thus, to understand all pre-Tannakian categories of moderate growth over
\Bbbk
, a possible route goes as follows:

(1)

Find all incompressible tensor categories
\mathcal{D}
of moderate growth.

(2)

For each
\mathcal{D}
as above, characterize intrinsically which pre-Tannakian categories
\mathcal{C}
admit a symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
.

Conjecturally, incompressible tensor categories (of moderate growth) are classified
[
2
,
5
]
. However, for the smallest incompressible categories known (even for
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
and
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
), a characterization of all
\mathcal{C}
as in part (
2
) is a hard problem.
To illustrate our point, consider the universal Verlinde category
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
, defined as the semisimplification of
\mathsf{Rep}_{\Bbbk}(\mathbb{Z}/p)
. This is the first term in a conjecturally exhaustive nested sequence of incompressible categories of moderate growth

\mathsf{Ver}_{p}\subset\mathsf{Ver}_{p^{2}}\subset\cdots\subset\mathsf{Ver}_{p^{n}}\subset\cdots

Now, given a pre-Tannakian category
\mathcal{C}
, the problem of deciphering intrinsically whether it admits a symmetric functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
was the motivating problem of the foundational work
[
24
]
. It was finally solved in
[
6
]
using
[
24
,
7
,
15
]
, among other novel tools and ideas; the problem turns out to be governed by the exactness of the
Frobenius functor
, see Section
2.3
for some details.

1.2.
Affine group schemes in pre-Tannakian categories

Since a complete solution for Problem (
2
) above seems out of reach, in this work we contribute to it through examples. By Tannaka-Krein duality
[
10
,
5
]
, for each pre-Tannakian target
\mathcal{D}
, any symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
endowed with a symmetric functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
comes from group scheme theory in
\mathcal{D}
. More precisely, any such
\mathcal{C}
can be uniquely obtained as
\mathsf{Rep}(G,\phi)
where
G
is an affine group scheme in
\mathcal{D}
equipped with a group map
\phi\colon\pi(\mathcal{D})\to G
that induces the canonical action of the fundamental group
\pi(\mathcal{D})
on
G
, and
\mathsf{Rep}(G,\phi)
denotes the representations of
G
where
\pi(\mathcal{D})
acts canonically after restriction along
\phi
.

A theory of affine group schemes (and their representations) in pre-Tannakian categories is starting to emerge. Precise results for group schemes in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
have been established by
[
27
,
28
]
, with a wider (yet detailed) study of the theory over more general pre-Tannakian categories by
[
8
]
.
However, group schemes in general symmetric categories are still not well understood through examples, and in this work we establish a first step towards a steady supply.

To the best of our knowledge, the only concrete examples constructed thus far are the general and special linear groups
\operatorname{GL}(X)
and
\operatorname{SL}(X)
over any object
X
of a symmetric category
1
1

1

It has recently been brought to our attention by Arun Kannan that orthogonal, symplectic and type-Q groups can also be defined in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
.
. Furthermore, when working on the category
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
, the irreducible representations of these groups are understood; all this progress was achieved in
[
28
,
29
]
, relying on a Harish-Chandra equivalence. This is where Lie algebras enter the picture.

The main result of
[
28
]
establishes an equivalence between affine group schemes of finite type in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
and Harish-Chandra pairs in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
.
Here a group
G
corresponds to the pair
(G_{0},\mathfrak{g})
, where
G_{0}\in\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}\subseteq\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
is an ordinary affine group underlying
G
and
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
is the Lie algebra of
G
. A possible interpretation of this result is that the main breach from group schemes in
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
to group schemes in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
is governed by Lie algebras in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
.

1.3.
Contragredient Lie superalgebras

On a more classical note, the predominant tool and source of examples towards a (still incomplete) classification of simple Lie algebras in
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
or
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
comes from what is known as the contragredient construction
[
20
]
. This process takes a square matrix
A
and a parity vector
\mathbf{p}
and defines, by generators and relations, the
contragredient
Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}(A,\mathbf{p})
, which possess many desired properties, such as a lattice grading and a generalized root system.
2
2

2

In the literature, contragredient algebras are also called algebras with a Cartan matrix, or Kac-Moody algebras. The latter is sometimes reserved for matrices that produce locally nilpotent generators.

The main contribution of this work is the construction of contragredient Lie algebras over arbitrary symmetric tensor categories. The first obvious obstacle when generalizing from
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
or
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
to a general symmetric category is the lack of elements. A second obstacle, that is not observed when upgrading the construction from
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
to
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, is the potential existence of new algebraic tori. To illustrate, recall that the construction of the contragredient superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}(A,\mathbf{p})
starts with (and depends on) a
realization
of the matrix
A
. Essentially, this is an abelian Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
in
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
playing the role of a Cartan subalgebra. The adjoint action of
\mathfrak{h}
on
\mathfrak{g}(A,\mathbf{p})
is then completely described by the matrix
A
. In contrast, already in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
, one encounters algebraic tori with a more sophisticated representation theory, see
[
29
]
.

1.4.
A summary of our construction and main results

Fix a symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
. Our construction takes as input a
contragredient datum
in
\mathcal{C}
as introduced in
Definition

4.1
. This is a triple
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
where
\mathtt{X}\in\mathcal{C}
is a Lie algebra,
\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V}
is an
\mathtt{X}
-module in
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathtt{d}\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{X}
is a map of
\mathtt{X}
-modules

3
3

3

In reminiscence to the classical case,
\mathtt{X}
should be thought as a placeholder for the Cartan subalgebra,
\mathtt{V}
is a substitute for the positive generators
e_{i}
’s and their parity, while
\rho
(the action of
\mathtt{X}
on
\mathtt{V}
) was formerly encoded by the matrix
A
; finally
\mathtt{d}
describes the bracket between
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
.
.
From this data we define first an auxiliary Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
as the minimal quotient of the semidirect product
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
where the bracket between
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
coincides with
\mathtt{d}
, see
Definition

4.8
. Our first main result is concerned with the structure of this algebra.

Theorem
4.18
.

Let
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as in
Definition

4.1
. Assume that
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules in the sense of
Definition

4.13
. Then, for the operadic Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\coloneq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
, the following hold:

(a)

There is a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k},

\displaystyle\text{such that }\deg\mathtt{V}^{*}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{X}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{V}

\displaystyle=1.

(b)

If
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
denote the subalgebras generated by
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
, respectively, then the natural maps
\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
induce Lie algebras isomorphisms
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\cong\mathtt{n}_{+}
and
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})\cong\mathtt{n}_{-}
.

(c)

We have
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}=\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
as objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k<0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k},

\displaystyle\mathtt{X}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{0},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k>0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}.

(d)

Among all
\mathbb{Z}
-homogeneous ideals of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
trivially intersecting
\mathtt{X}
, there exists a unique maximal one
\mathtt{m}
. Moreover,
\mathtt{m}=\mathtt{m}_{+}\oplus\mathtt{m}_{-}
, where
\mathtt{m}_{\pm}=\mathtt{m}\cap\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{\pm}
.

Now we arrive at our main definition: the contragredient Lie algebra
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
associated to
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
is the quotient
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})/\mathtt{m}
, see
Definition

4.30
. Under a mild assumption on the pair
(\rho,\mathtt{d})
, we obtain in
Theorem

4.33
the first structural properties for
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
. In particular, it inherits from
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading and a triangular decomposition
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})=\mathtt{n}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\mathtt{n}_{+}
, where
\mathtt{n}_{+}
and
\mathtt{n}_{-}
are the subalgebras generated by
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
.

To obtain further properties, we restrict to a special source of contragredient data, that we call symmetrizable and introduce in
Definition

4.41
. The main difference here is the assumption that
\mathtt{X}
comes with an invariant non-degenerate form
\mathtt{K}\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{X}\to\mathds{1}
. Then, for any module
\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V}
, one can produce a map
\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}}\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{X}
of
\mathtt{X}
-modules.

Our first main result show that, from any
\mathtt{X}
-module decomposition
\mathtt{V}=\oplus_{1\leq i\leq r}\mathtt{V}_{i}
, one obtains for
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}})
a grading by the lattice
Q\coloneq\oplus_{1\leq i\leq r}\alpha_{i}\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem
4.49
.

The Lie algebra
\mathtt{g}\coloneq\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}})
admits a
Q
-grading such that

\displaystyle\mathtt{g}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in Q}\mathtt{g}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{X}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{V}_{i}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i},

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{V}_{i}^{*}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{i},

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq i\leq r.

Moreover, under this grading we have

\displaystyle\mathtt{g}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{X},

\displaystyle\mathtt{n}_{+}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in Q^{+},\alpha\neq 0}\mathtt{g}_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathtt{n}_{-}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in Q^{-},\alpha\neq 0}\mathtt{g}_{\alpha}.

In our second main theorem we show that any decomposition for
\mathtt{V}
as above yields a non-degenerate invariant form on
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}})
.

Theorem
4.53
.

The algebra
\mathtt{g}
admits an invariant symmetric form
\mathtt{B}\colon\mathtt{g}\otimes\mathtt{g}\to\mathds{1}
such that

(a)

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{B}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{X}}}=\mathtt{K}
,

(b)

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{B}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{g}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathtt{g}_{\beta}}}=0
if
\alpha+\beta\neq 0
, and

(c)

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{B}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{g}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathtt{g}_{-\alpha}}}
is non-degenerate for all
\alpha\in Q
.

We end the paper with examples of contragredient Lie algebras, mostly in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
, coming from three sources. First, we recover general and special Lie algebras
\mathfrak{gl}(V)
,
\mathfrak{sl}(V)
over an object
V\in\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
as contragredient Lie algebras. Then we go back to our previous work
[
1
]
, where we studied Lie algebras in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
obtained by semisimplification (following
[
21
]
); we show in
Section

5.2
that these examples are also contragredient.

Finally, in
Section

5.3
we describe all contragredient data over the torus
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathtt{L}_{2})\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathsf{Ver}_{p})
and its subalgebras
\mathds{1}
and
\mathfrak{sl}(\mathtt{L}_{2})
, assuming that the positive part is a simple object of
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
. For the torus
\mathtt{X}=\mathds{1}
we are able to compute the corresponding contragredient Lie algebras, obtaining in
Proposition

5.9
previously unknown examples of Lie algebras in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
. These examples can be thought of as generalizations of the rank-one Lie superalgebras with non-trivial action of
\mathds{1}
, namely
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{osp}(2|1)
, see
Remark

5.11
.
