2.
Preliminaries

Conventions.

We denote by
\mathbb{N}
the set of positive integers and
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
.

Fix an algebraically closed field
\Bbbk
of characteristic
p\geq 0
.
We denote by
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
and
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
the symmetric fusion categories of finite dimensional vector spaces and super vector spaces over
\Bbbk
, respectively.

In this work, all categories are assumed essentially small. When working on an additive category
\mathcal{C}
, we denote by
\oplus\colon\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
the biproduct bifunctor. Consider arrows
f_{1}\colon X_{1}\to Y_{1}
and
f_{2}\colon X_{2}\to Y_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
, then

•

we reserve
f_{1}\oplus f_{2}\colon X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\to Y_{1}\oplus Y_{2}
to denote the biproduct arrow;

•

if
X_{1}=X_{2}=X
we denote the product arrow by
(f_{1},f_{2})\colon X\to Y_{1}\oplus Y_{2}
;

•

if
Y_{1}=Y_{2}=Y
, we use
f_{1}\sqcup f_{2}\colon X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\to Y
to denote the coproduct arrow;

•

if
X_{1}=X_{2}=X
and
Y_{1}=Y_{2}=Y
, we use
f_{1}\pm f_{2}
to denote the arrows obtained from the group structure of
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
.

2.1.
Symmetric tensor categories and Deligne’s Theorem

A
(symmetric) tensor category
is an abelian
\Bbbk
-linear category which admits a rigid (symmetric) monoidal structure in a compatible way: the endomorphism space of the unit object is one-dimensional and the tensor product functor is
\Bbbk
-bilinear on morphisms (thus exact by rigidity
[
12
]
*Proposition 4.2.1). Notice that we adopt the conventions of
[
6
]
which differ from those of
[
10
,
11
,
12
]
.

Given an object
X
in an abelian category
\mathcal{C}
, the
length

\ell(X)
of
X
is the supremum of the set of integers
m
for which
X
admits an strictly increasing family of subobjects
0\subsetneq X_{1}\subsetneq\dots\subsetneq X_{m}=X
in
\mathcal{C}
.

A
pre-Tannakian
category is a symmetric tensor category wherein all objects have finite length. Under this assumption all Hom spaces turn out to be finite dimensional.

Throughout this work, tensor functors are assumed (or required) to be exact. More precisely, a
(symmetric) tensor functor
is an exact
\Bbbk
-linear (symmetric) monoidal functor between (symmetric) tensor categories. Notice that, for a monoidal
\Bbbk
-linear functor between tensor categories, exactness is equivalent to faithfulness thanks to
[
10
]
*Corollaire 2.10 (i) and
[
4
]
*Theorem 2.4.1.

We sometimes say that a pre-Tannakian category
\mathcal{C}

fibers over
some other pre-Tannakian category
\mathcal{D}
if there is a symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
. This terminology fits well with the classical one (e.g. that from
[
26
]
) thanks to
[
5
]
*§4, where Tannaka-Krein duality over a pre-Tannakian target is established. Roughly speaking, this means that both
\mathcal{C}
and the fiber functor can be uniquely recovered from group-scheme theory in
\mathcal{D}
.

2.1.1.
Semisimplification of symmetric tensor categories

Consider a
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal category
\mathcal{C}
. A tensor ideal
\mathcal{I}
in
\mathcal{C}
is a collection of subspaces
\mathcal{I}=\{\mathcal{I}(X,Y)\subseteq\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\}
, where
X,Y
are objects in
\mathcal{C}
, which is compatible with compositions and tensor products. This enables us to form the
quotient category
of
\mathcal{C}
by
\mathcal{I}
, which has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
, but the new spaces of morphisms are the quotients
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)/\mathcal{I}(X,Y)
. This quotient procedure is compatible with the
\Bbbk
-linear monoidal structure (among many other properties and structures) of the original category.

An special instance of this constructions starts with an spherical category
\mathcal{C}

[
12
]
*§4.7 and considers the tensor ideal of
negligible
objects. In this case the quotient, known as the
semisimplification
of
\mathcal{C}
, turns out to be semisimple, where the simples are the indecomposables in
\mathcal{C}
with non-zero categorical dimension. See
[
16
]
and references therein.

2.1.2.
Ind-completions of symmetric tensor categories

For a symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
its
ind-completion
, which is defined to be the closure of
\mathcal{C}
under filtered colimits.
Since the tensor product of
\mathcal{C}
is exact, it preserves colimits and induces a tensor product on
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
. By naturality,
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
inherits the braiding from
\mathcal{C}
. Hence
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
is a
\Bbbk
-linear abelian category with an exact and symmetric tensor product, and there is an exact symmetric embedding
\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
that is universal in some sense, see
[
22
]
.
We shall refer to the objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
as ind-objects of
\mathcal{C}
. The genuine objects of
\mathcal{C}
are then recovered as ind-objects of finite length. If its clear from the context that
X
and
Y
are ind-objects, we write
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
instead of
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}}(X,Y)
.
As an example, if
\mathcal{C}
is moreover fusion, that is, finite and semisimple, then the objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
are (possibly infinite) direct sums of simple objects in
\mathcal{C}
.

2.1.3.
Vanishing of alternating powers

Given a positive integer
n
and an object
X
in a symmetric tensor category, the
n
-th alternating power
\operatorname{A}^{n}(X)
is defined as the image of
\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}(-1)^{\operatorname{sign}(\sigma)}\sigma\colon X^{\otimes n}\to X^{\otimes n}
. Here we follow the terminology of
[
10
]
, and point out that there are other possible definitions of alternating powers of
X
in
\mathcal{C}
, which are equivalent in
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
but differ already in
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
if
p>2
, see
[
14
]
*§2.1.
A first version of Deligne’s Theorem states that, in characteristic
0
, vanishing of alternating powers detects Tannakian categories (those of the form
\mathsf{Rep}(G)
for an affine group scheme
G
) among all pre-Tannakian categories.

Theorem 2.1
.

[
10
,
11
]
Assume
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk=0
and let
\mathcal{C}
be a pre-Tannakian category over
\Bbbk
. Then
\mathcal{C}
admits a symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
if and only if for each
X
in
\mathcal{C}
there is some
n\geq 1
such that
\operatorname{A}^{n}(X)=0
.

2.1.4.
Moderate growth

Consider a tensor category
\mathcal{C}
where all objects have finite length. Then
\mathcal{C}
has
moderate growth
if for each object
X
there is some real number
a_{X}
so that
\ell(X^{\otimes n})\leq a_{X}^{n}
for all
n\geq 1
.
Famously, if
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk=0
, this growth condition detects categories of finite-dimensional representations of affine proalgebraic supergroups among all pre-Tannakian categories. More precisely:

Theorem 2.2
.

[
11
]
Assume
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk=0
and let
\mathcal{C}
be a pre-Tannakian category over
\Bbbk
. Then
\mathcal{C}
admits a symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
if and only if it has moderate growth.

It is well known (see e.g.
[
18
,
19
,
27
]
) that this characterization fails to hold over fields of positive characteristic, where the problem of obtaining analogue statements remained somewhat unexplored until Ostrik’s recent breakthrough
[
24
]
. From the moment of its appearance,
[
24
]
motivated and inspired many other remarkable works concerning symmetric tensor categories of moderate growth over fields of positive characteristic. In the next Sections, we discuss this still unveiling story.

2.2.
The Verlinde category and Ostrik’s Theorem

The first Frobenius kernel
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}
of the additive group scheme over a field of characteristic
p>0
is represented by the Hopf algebra
\Bbbk[t]/(t^{p})
where
t
is primitive. By self-duality, indecomposables in
\mathsf{Rep}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p})
are parametrized, up to isomorphism, by Jordan blocks
L_{i}
of size
i
with
1\leq i\leq p
.

By definition, the Verlinde category
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
is the semisimplification of the symmetric tensor category
\mathsf{Rep}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p})
, see
[
18
,
19
,
24
]
. Hence,
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
is a symmetric fusion category equipped with a
\Bbbk
-linear symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{S}\colon\mathsf{Rep}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p})\to\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
. Simple objects of
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
are given, up to isomorphism, by the images
\mathtt{L}_{i}
of the indecomposables
L_{i}
with
1\leq i\leq p-1
of
\mathsf{Rep}(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p})
, and the monoidal unit is
\mathds{1}=\mathtt{L}_{1}
. Non-faithfulness of the semisimplification functor becomes evident by looking at the indecomposable
L_{p}
, which is zero-quantum-dimensional and thus annihilated. In
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
all simple objects are self-dual, and the fusion rules are given by the truncated Clebsch-Gordan formula

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathtt{L}_{i}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{j}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{\min\{i,j,p-i,p-j\}}\mathtt{L}_{|i-j|+2k-1},

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq i,j\leq p-1.

Notice that
\mathsf{Ver}_{2}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
and
\mathsf{Ver}_{3}=\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
. Furthermore, for any
p\geq 3
, the abelian subcategory of
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
generated by
\mathtt{L}_{1}
and
\mathtt{L}_{p-1}
is a fusion category that turns out to be equivalent, as a symmetric tensor category, to
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, see
[
24
,
21
]
.

On the other hand, when
p\geq 5
, the fusion rules (
2.3
) imply that the abelian subcategory of
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
spanned by simples
\mathtt{L}_{i}
with
i
odd is again a fusion subcategory, denoted by
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}^{+}
. By
[
24
]
, the subcategory
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}^{+}
is tensor generated by
\mathtt{L}_{3}
, and we have
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}\cong\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}\boxtimes\mathsf{Ver}_{p}^{+}
as symmetric fusion categories.

By a dimension argument, as long as
p\geq 5
, neither
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
nor
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}^{+}
fiber over
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
. For instance, when
p=5
, the object
\mathtt{L}_{3}
satisfies
\mathtt{L}_{3}^{\otimes 2}=\mathds{1}\oplus\mathtt{L}_{3}
. The next result is in some sense the starting point of the modern theory of symmetric tensor categories of moderate growth.

Theorem 2.4
.

[
24
]
Assume
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk=p>0
. Then any symmetric fusion category
\mathcal{C}
over
\Bbbk
admits a symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
.

We compute for later use the self braiding of the simple objects
\mathtt{L}_{i}
in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
1\leq i<p
. With respect to the fixed decomposition (
2.3
), the symmetry
c:=c_{\mathtt{L}_{i}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{i}}
is given by the diagonal matrix
\mathrm{diag}\left((-1)^{i-k}\right)_{1\leq k\leq\min\{i,p-i\}}
. In other words,

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2ptc\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{L}_{2k-1}}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i-k}\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{L}_{2k-1}},

\displaystyle 1\leq k\leq\min\{i,p-i\}.

Proof.

Take first
i\leq\frac{p-1}{2}
. Fix a cyclic basis
(v_{j})_{1\leq j\leq i}
of the indecomposable
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}
-module
L_{i}
, which means that
t\cdot v_{j}=v_{j+1}
if
j<i
and
t\cdot v_{i}=0
. On the other hand, consider
M\coloneq\Bbbk[x,y]/(x^{i},y^{i})
; since
\overline{(x+y)^{p}}=\overline{x^{p}+y^{p}}=0
, the rule
t\cdot f=(x+y)f
defines an
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}
-module structure on
M
. Furthermore, the map
L_{i}\otimes L_{i}\to M
,
v_{j}\otimes v_{k}\mapsto x^{j-1}y^{k-1}
, is an isomorphism of
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}
-modules. We decompose
M
to compute the self braiding of
L_{i}
.

Let
M_{S}
and
M_{A}
denote the subspaces of symmetric and antisymmetric polynomials modulo
(x^{i},y^{i})
, respectively. Thus
M=M_{S}\oplus M_{A}
as
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}
-modules. We decompose further

\displaystyle M_{S}

\displaystyle=L_{j_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{j_{r}},

\displaystyle M_{A}

\displaystyle=L_{j_{r+1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{j_{i}},

\displaystyle j_{1}>\cdots>j_{r},

\displaystyle\,j_{r+1}>\cdots>j_{i},

as sums of indecomposable submodules, with each
L_{j}
generated by
v_{j}
as
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{p}
-module, so
\{j_{1},\dots,j_{i}\}=\{2k-1\colon 1\leq k\leq i\}
. Polynomial degree in
\Bbbk[x,y]
induces an
\mathbb{N}_{0}
-graded algebra structure on
M
, say

M=\oplus_{0\leq k\leq 2i-2}M_{k}
, which satisfies
t\cdot M_{k}\subseteq M_{k+1}
. Let
\pi_{k}\colon M\twoheadrightarrow M_{k}
be the canonical projection. As
M_{A}\subseteq\oplus_{1\leq k\leq 2i-2}M_{k}
, we have that
t^{2i-2}\cdot M_{A}=0
. Thus
j_{1}=2i-1
, with
\pi_{0}(v_{j_{1}})\neq 0
. As
M_{S}\cap M_{1}
and
M_{S}\cap M_{2i-2}
are one-dimensional, since
0\neq t\cdot v_{j_{1}}\in M_{2t-2}
, we have that
L_{j_{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{j_{k}}\subseteq\oplus_{2\leq k\leq 2i-3}M_{k}
, so
t^{2i-4}
annihilates these summands, which implies that
j_{r+1}=2i-3
, and
\pi_{1}(v_{j_{r+1}})\neq 0
. Similarly,
L_{j_{r+2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus L_{j_{i}}\subseteq\oplus_{3\leq k\leq 2i-4}M_{k}
, so
j_{2}=2i-5
. A similar argument shows recursively that
r=\tfrac{i}{2}
if
i
is even and
r=\tfrac{i+1}{2}
if
i
is odd, with

\displaystyle j_{1}

\displaystyle=2i-1,j_{2}=2i-5,\dots,j_{r}=\begin{cases}3&i\text{ even},\\
1&i\text{ odd},\end{cases}

\displaystyle j_{r+1}

\displaystyle=2i-3,\dots,j_{i}=\begin{cases}1&i\text{ even},\\
3&i\text{ odd},\end{cases}

and the statement follows by applying the semisimplification functor
\mathsf{S}
.

Consider now
i\geq\frac{p-1}{2}
. The isomorphism
\mathtt{L}_{i}\simeq\mathtt{L}_{p-1}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{p-i}
induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathtt{L}_{i}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{i}\simeq\mathtt{L}_{p-1}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{p-i}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{p-1}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{p-i}

Using the naturality of the symmetry,
{\left.\kern-1.2ptc\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{L}_{2k-1}}}
is given by
the restriction of
c_{\mathtt{L}_{p-1}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{p-i},\mathtt{L}_{p-1}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{p-i}}
composed with
c_{\mathtt{L}_{p-1},\mathtt{L}_{p-1}}=-\operatorname{id}_{L_{1}}
, so the statement follows from the first case.
∎

2.3.
Frobenius exact categories and Coulembier-Etingof-Ostrik’s Theorem

One of the main contributions of
[
24
]
is the construction of the
Frobenius functor

\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{(1)}\boxtimes\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
, which comes as a generalization of the pullback
\mathsf{Rep}(G)\to\mathsf{Rep}(G)^{(1)}
along the Frobenius map
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{p}
for an affine group scheme
G
. A priori, the Frobenius functor in its original form
[
24
]
was only available for
semisimple
pre-Tannakian categories. The task of making it available in a more general setting immediately garnered relevance, and was independently achieved in
[
7
,
15
]
, producing slightly different versions of a
\Bbbk
-linear functor
\operatorname{Fr}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{(1)}\boxtimes\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
for any pre-Tannakian category
\mathcal{C}
. These approaches were unified in
[
6
]
. It turns out that
Frobenius exactness
(i.e. exactness of
\operatorname{Fr}
) is the unique novel obstruction for a pre-Tannakian category to fiber over
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
.

Theorem 2.6
.

[
6
]

Assume
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk=p>0
. Then, for a pre-Tannakian category
\mathcal{C}
, the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{C}
admits a symmetric tensor functor
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
;

(2)

\mathcal{C}
is Frobenius exact and of moderate growth;

(3)

\mathcal{C}
is Frobenius exact and for each object
X
in
\mathcal{C}
there is some
n\geq 1
so that
\operatorname{A}^{n}(X)=0
.

A curious byproduct of this result is that Deligne’s conditions for Tannakian and super-Tannakian categories in characteristic
0
(see
Theorems

2.1
and
2.2
) are actually equivalent for Frobenius exact categories in positive characteristic.

2.4.
Operadic, PBW, and genuine Lie algebras

Following
[
17
]
an
(ind-)operadic Lie algebra
over a (strict) symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
is an (ind-)object
\mathfrak{g}
in
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a morphism
b\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
that satisfies the usual anti-symmetric and Jacobi conditions:

(2.7)

\displaystyle b\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}+c_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}})=0,\qquad b\circ(b\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}}+(123)_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}}+(132)_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}})=0,

where
(123)_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}},(132)_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}}\colon\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}\to\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 3}
are obtained from the action of the symmetric group. We use
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})
to denote the category of operadic Lie algebras in
\mathcal{C}
. When no confusion is possible, we use the notation
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})
also for
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}})
. Note that in previous bibliography, such as
[
25
]
, operadic Lie algebras are just called Lie algebras; however, a distinction needs to be made, especially when working in positive characteristic.

Recall from
[
17
]
that the
universal enveloping algebra

\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
is the quotient of the tensor algebra
\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})
by the two-sided ideal generated by the image of the map

(2.8)

\displaystyle a\coloneq(-b,\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}-c_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}}):\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\subseteq\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g}).

Similarly, the symmetric algebra
\operatorname{S}(\mathfrak{g})
is the quotient of
\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})
by the image of
\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}-c_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}}
.
As usual, there is a natural map
\eta\colon\operatorname{S}(\mathfrak{g})\to\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
of ind-algebras in
\mathcal{C}
. However, it can fail to be an isomorphism, which is the main reason behind the adjective
operadic
in the definition above.
This somewhat undesired behavior was already exhibited by
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
when
p=2
: for
\eta
to be an isomorphism one needs to impose the additional condition
b(x\otimes x)=0
for all
x
. Similarly, in
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
over
p=3
, one needs to assume
b(b(x\otimes x)\otimes x)=0
for all odd
x
.

Definition 2.9
.

[
17
]

We say that an operadic Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is
PBW
if the natural map
\eta\colon\operatorname{S}(\mathfrak{g})\to\operatorname{gr}\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
is an isomorphism.

2.4.1.
Free operadic Lie algebras

By
[
17
]
*Section 4.2, one can build a free operadic Lie algebra over any object in a symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
. More precisely, there is a functor
\operatorname{FOLie}
from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
), the category of ind-operadic Lie algebras in
\mathcal{C}
, which is left adjoint to the forgetful functor. That is, we have a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,L)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}})}(\operatorname{FOLie}(V),L),

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{C},\ L\in\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C}).

This notion plays a fundamental role in the theory of Lie algebras in positive characteristic. For instance, it is used in
[
17
]
*Proposition 6.10 to construct in
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
(for any characteristic
p\geq 5
), an operadic Lie algebra (of finite length) that is not PBW.
More importantly, the notion of genuine Lie algebra in a symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
introduced in
[
17
]
relies heavily on this construction, as we briefly summarize next.

Given any object
V
in
\mathcal{C}
, the inclusion of
V
into
\operatorname{T}(V)
–which becomes an operadic Lie algebra via the commutator– gives rise to a map
\phi^{V}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})}(\operatorname{FOLie}(V),\operatorname{T}(V))
.
As in
[
17
]
*§4.2 we write
\phi^{V}=\oplus_{n\geq 1}\phi^{V}_{n}
, where
\phi^{V}_{n}:\operatorname{FOLie}_{n}(V)\to V^{\otimes n}
, and denote

(2.10)

\displaystyle E_{n}(V)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ker\phi^{V}_{n},\quad n\geq 1,

\displaystyle E(V)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\ker\phi^{V}=\oplus_{n\geq 1}E_{n}(V).

On the other hand, for an operadic Lie algebra
L
in
\mathcal{C}
let

\beta^{L}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})}(\operatorname{FOLie}(L),L)

denote the map corresponding to the identity map
\operatorname{id}_{L}
in
\mathcal{C}
under the adjunction.

Definition 2.11
.

[
17
]

An operadic Lie algebra
L
in a symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
is a
Lie algebra
if
\beta^{L}
annihilates
E(L)
.
We denote by
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
the category of Lie algebras in
\mathcal{C}
.

Verifying if a given operadic Lie algebra in a tensor category is either PBW or a genuine Lie algebra is rather involved. Fortunately, in
[
17
]
it is shown that the former implies the latter, and more generally, that any associative algebra and its subobjects closed under the commutator are Lie algebras. Preservation of these properties under quotients and subobjects is also studied.

The situation is better understood for
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
, and hence for any Frobenius exact pre-Tannakian category of moderate growth. Indeed,
[
17
]
introduces for any
p\geq 5
the
p
-Jacobi identity
, a rather explicit relation of degree
p
that generalizes the conditions
b(x\otimes x)=0
and
b(b(x\otimes x)\otimes x)=0
needed for characteristics
2
and
3
, respectively. Namely,

Definition 2.12
.

[
17
]
We say
an operadic Lie algebra
L
in a symmetric tensor category satisfies the
p
-Jacobi identity if
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\beta^{L}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E_{p}(L)}}=0
.

Theorem 2.13
.

[
17
]
*Theorem 6.6, Corollary 6.7,
[
6
]
*Theorem 1.1
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a Frobenius exact pre-Tannakian category of moderate growth. An operadic Lie algebra in
\mathcal{C}
is PBW if and only if it is a Lie algebra, if and only if it satisfies the
p
-Jacobi identity.

2.4.2.
Free Lie algebras

Using free operadic Lie algebras, one can construct the functor

\displaystyle\operatorname{FLie}

\displaystyle\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{FLie}(V)

\displaystyle\coloneq\operatorname{FOLie}(V)/\ker\left(\phi^{V}\right),

which is left adjoint to the forgetful functor by
[
17
]
*Corollary 4.9. Thus for any object
V
we have the free Lie algebra
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
, which is an ind-Lie subalgebra of
\operatorname{T}(V)
.

Proposition 2.14
.

The free Lie algebra
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
is naturally isomorphic to the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{f}
of
\operatorname{T}(V)
generated by
V
. The inclusion
\mathfrak{f}\hookrightarrow\operatorname{T}(V)
extends to an isomorphism of algebras
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{f})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{T}(V)
. In particular,
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
is PBW.

Proof.

Note first that
\mathfrak{f}
is PBW by
[
17
]
*Proposition 4.7. Let
\iota_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon V\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{f}
denote the inclusion. We show that for any
L\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
and
\psi\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,L)
, there is a unique
\overline{\psi}\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})}(\mathfrak{f},L)
such that
\overline{\psi}\iota_{\mathfrak{f}}=\psi
.
By the universal property of
\operatorname{T}(V)
there is a unique algebra map
\overline{\psi}\colon\operatorname{T}(V)\to\operatorname{U}(L)
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{T}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{U}(L).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{L}}

Thus the image of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\overline{\psi}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{V}}
is contained in
L
. Since
\overline{\psi}
is an algebra map and
\mathfrak{f}
is generated by
V
, this implies that the image of
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\overline{\psi}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{f}}}
is also contained in
L
. Hence we have a Lie algebra map
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\overline{\psi}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{f}}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\to L
such that
\overline{\psi}\iota_{\mathfrak{f}}=\psi
, as desired. Uniqueness is clear, so the first statement follows.

For the second statement, given an associative algebra
A
and
\phi\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})}(\mathfrak{f},A)
, the restriction
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\phi\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{V}}
can be extended uniquely to a map of associative algebras
\overline{\phi}\colon\operatorname{T}(V)\to A
. Then one shows as before that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\overline{\phi}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{f}}}=\phi
, and the claim follows.
∎

Remark 2.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra in
\mathcal{C}
, and
V
a subobject of
\mathfrak{g}
. The subalgebra generated by
V
is
\langle V\rangle=\sum_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}
, where
\varphi_{1}:V\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}
is just the inclusion and
\varphi_{n+1}\coloneq{\left.\kern-1.2ptb\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes V}}
.

This description applies to the pair
(V,\mathfrak{g})=(V,\operatorname{T}(V))
, and shows that
\mathfrak{f}\simeq\operatorname{FLie}(V)
is a graded Lie algebra
\mathfrak{f}=\oplus_{n\geq 1}\mathfrak{f}_{n}
, as
\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\subseteq V^{\otimes n}
. Here,
\mathfrak{f}_{1}=V
,
\mathfrak{f}_{2}=\operatorname{im}(\operatorname{id}_{V\otimes V}-c_{V,V})
.

Remark 2.16
.

Consider
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
and a simple object
V=\mathtt{L}_{i}
. We have a decomposition
\mathtt{L}_{i}\otimes\mathtt{L}_{i}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{\min\{i,p-i\}}\mathtt{L}_{2k-1}
, and by
Lemma

2.5
, we get

\displaystyle\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{L}_{i})_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\ \text{antisymmetric}}\mathtt{L}_{2k-1}^{(2)},

where the sum runs over indexes
1\leq k\leq\min\{i,p-i\}
such that the self-braiding
c_{\mathtt{L}_{i},\mathtt{L}_{i}}
acts as
-1
on
\mathtt{L}_{2k-1}
.
In particular,
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{L}_{i})_{2}
contains a copy of
\mathds{1}=\mathtt{L}_{1}
if and only if
i
is even.

2.5.
Contragredient Lie (super)algebras

Here we recall the basics of contragredient Lie superalgebras; we refer to
[
3
,
20
]
for details.
The starting input data is:

(D1)

a square matrix
A=(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq r}
with entries in
\Bbbk
;

(D2)

a
parity vector

\mathbf{p}=(p_{i})\in(\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})^{r}
.

Up to isomorphism, there is a unique
realization
of
A
, which consists on

(D3)

a
\Bbbk
-vector space
\mathfrak{h}
of dimension
2r-\operatorname{rank}A
;

(D4)

linearly independent subsets
(\xi_{i})_{1\leq i\leq r}\subset\mathfrak{h}^{*}
and
(h_{i})_{1\leq i\leq r}\subset\mathfrak{h}
,
such that
\xi_{j}(h_{i})=a_{ij}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq r
.

From this data, we introduce first the auxiliary Lie superalgebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\coloneq\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}(A,\mathbf{p})
generated by elements
e_{i}
,
f_{i}
for
1\leq i,j\leq r
, and
\mathfrak{h}
,
with parity given by

\displaystyle|e_{i}|

\displaystyle=|f_{i}|=p_{i},\quad 1\leq i\leq r,

\displaystyle|h|

\displaystyle=0,\mbox{ for all }h\in\mathfrak{h},

modulo the relations

(2.17)

\displaystyle[h,h^{\prime}]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[h,e_{i}]

\displaystyle=\xi_{i}(h)e_{i},

\displaystyle[h,f_{i}]

\displaystyle=-\xi_{i}(h)f_{i},

\displaystyle[e_{i},f_{j}]

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}h_{i}

for all
1\leq i,j\leq r
, and
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{h}
.

The Lie superalgebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
admits a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading with
\deg f_{i}=-1
,
\deg\mathfrak{h}=0
and
\deg e_{i}=1
. Moreover,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
contains a unique maximal graded ideal
\mathfrak{m}
among those that meet
\mathfrak{h}
trivially.

The
contragredient Lie superalgebra
associated to the pair
(A,\mathbf{p})
is the Lie superalgebra quotient
\mathfrak{g}(A,\mathbf{p})\coloneq\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}(A,\mathbf{p})/\mathfrak{m}
.
This superalgebra canonically contains the
e_{i}
’s,
f_{i}
’s, and
\mathfrak{h}
. It also inherits a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading
\mathfrak{g}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathfrak{g}_{k}
and a triangular decomposition

\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{n}_{-}\oplus\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{n}_{+}
where
\mathfrak{n}_{-}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{+}
are the Lie subsuperalgebras generated by the
e_{i}
’s and the
f_{i}
’s, respectively.

The
\mathbb{Z}
-grading above can be refined to a
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-grading. Indeed,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-graded with

\displaystyle\deg f_{i}

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{i},

\displaystyle\deg h

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\deg e_{i}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i},

\displaystyle\mbox{ for }1\leq i\leq r

\displaystyle\mbox{ and }h\in\mathfrak{h},

where
(\alpha_{i})
denotes the canonical basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
. The defining ideal
\mathfrak{m}
happens to be
\mathbb{Z}^{r}
-homogeneous, so
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{r},\alpha\neq 0}\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
.
We define the set of roots of
(A,\mathbf{p})
by

\nabla^{(A,\mathbf{p})}:=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{r}-0\colon\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\neq 0\},

and consider as usual the subsets of positive and negative roots:
\nabla_{\hskip 2.0pt\pm}^{(A,\mathbf{p})}=\nabla^{(A,\mathbf{p})}\cap(\pm\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{I}}_{0})
.

3.
Constructions for operadic Lie algebras in symmetric tensor categories

Fix a strict symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
. Essentially all constructions that are known for (operadic) Lie algebras in
\mathsf{Vec}_{\Bbbk}
or
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
should have a counterpart in our setting. Here we include some of these for completeness.

3.1.
Modules over operadic Lie algebras

With the goal of laying the foundations needed for our construction in
Section

4
, we develop some abstract nonsense representation theory for operadic Lie algebras in symmetric categories. A more in-depth analysis for the representation theory of affine group schemes in pre-Tannakian categories, towards the development of commutative algebra over incompressible categories, is available in
[
28
,
8
]
.

Following e.g.
[
25
]
*§2.2, a module over
\mathfrak{g}
is an object
V
in
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a map
\eta_{V}\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes V\to V
for which the following diagram commutes

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\otimes V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}\otimes V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\otimes V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}\otimes V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}-c_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}})\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\eta_{V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{V}}

Remark 3.2
.

Following
[
29
]
*§3, the
general Lie algebra
over an object
V\in\mathcal{C}
is the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}(V)
attached to the associative algebra
V\otimes V^{*}
with multiplication
\operatorname{id}_{V}\otimes\mathsf{ev}_{V}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{*}}
. By
[
29
]
*Proposition 3.12, Lie algebra maps
\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{gl}(V)
are in correspondence with
\mathfrak{g}
-module structures on
V
.

Let
W
be another
\mathfrak{g}
-module. A morphism of
\mathfrak{g}
-modules from
V
to
W
is a map
f\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(V,W)
that commutes with the actions of
\mathfrak{g}
. The set of such morphisms is denoted by
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(V,W)
as usual. The tensor product
V\otimes W\in\mathcal{C}
is also a
\mathfrak{g}
-module with action

(3.3)

\displaystyle\eta_{V\otimes W}\coloneqq\eta_{V}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W}+(\operatorname{id}_{V}\otimes\eta_{W})(c_{\mathfrak{g},V}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W}).

The dual
V^{*}
is also a
\mathfrak{g}
-module with action
\eta_{V^{*}}:\mathfrak{g}\otimes V^{*}\to V^{*}
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\eta_{V^{*}}

\displaystyle=-(\mathsf{ev}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{*}})(\operatorname{id}_{V^{*}}\otimes\eta\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V^{*}})(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes V^{*}}\otimes\mathsf{coev})c_{\mathfrak{g},V^{*}}.

Notice that
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathds{1}
are
\mathfrak{g}
-modules with
\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}=b
,
\eta_{\mathds{1}}=0
; and combining this with (
3.4
),
\mathfrak{g}^{*}
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module. The maps
\mathsf{ev}
and
\mathsf{coev}
are morphisms. Moreover, by
[
25
]
*Proposition 2 the family of all
\mathfrak{g}
-modules is a tensor category with unit
(\mathds{1},\eta_{\mathds{1}})
. We denote it by
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Remark 3.5
.

As usually,
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{g})
is canonically equivalent, as an abelian category, to
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g}))
, the category of modules in
\mathcal{C}
for the associative algebra
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})\in\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
.

Indeed, any
\mathfrak{g}
-action
\eta\colon V\otimes\mathfrak{g}\to V
as above, induces a map
\eta\colon\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes V\to V
endowing
V
with the structure of a module over the associative algebra
\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})
. Here
\eta_{n}\coloneq{\left.\kern-1.2pt\eta\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes n}\otimes V}}
is defined recursively by taking
\eta_{0}\colon\mathds{1}\otimes V\to V
as the unit isomorphism, and
\eta_{n}=\eta_{n-1}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes n-1}}\otimes\eta)
for
n>0
.
Since
V
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module, this map descends to the quotient
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes V\to V
, and endows
V
with a canonical
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
-module structure.

Conversely, a
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
-module becomes a
\mathfrak{g}
-module via pullback along the natural map
\mathfrak{g}\to\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
.

By
[
23
]
*Proposition 2.5 together with Remark
3.5
, we can conclude that
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{g})
is a tensor category by proving that
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
is a Hopf algebra.
Let
\iota\colon\mathds{1}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})
be the unit map. As in
[
28
]
*Definition 5.27,
\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})
is a Hopf algebra, where the comultiplication
\Delta\colon\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})\to\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})
is the unique algebra map such that

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Delta\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\iota+\iota\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}.

Clearly
\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{g})
is a cocommutative Hopf algebra.

Lemma 3.6
.

The map
\Delta
descends to an algebra map
\Delta\colon\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})\to\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
, and this makes
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
a cocommutative Hopf algebra.

Proof.

As for usual Lie algebras,

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Delta\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}}\otimes\iota+(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}}+c)\iota\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}}:\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}\to\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathds{1}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\oplus\mathds{1}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2},

and by direct computation,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\Delta\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes 2}}}\circ a=a\otimes\iota+\iota\otimes a
. Thus the ideal generated by the image of
a
is a bi-ideal. The antipode on
T(V)
descends to an antipode on
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Let
V,W\in\mathcal{C}
and
\overline{\eta}:V\otimes W\to W
a map.

(i)

W
is a
\operatorname{T}(V)
-module, with action
\eta=\oplus_{n\geq 0}\eta_{n}\colon\operatorname{T}(V)\otimes W\to W
given by

\displaystyle\eta_{n}

\displaystyle:V^{\otimes n}\otimes W\to W,

\displaystyle\eta_{1}

\displaystyle=\overline{\eta},

\displaystyle\eta_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\eta_{n}(\operatorname{id}_{V^{\otimes n}}\otimes\overline{\eta})

(ii)

By
Proposition

2.14
,
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
can be viewed as a Lie subalgebra of
\operatorname{T}(V)
. The restriction of
\eta
to
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
makes
W
a module over
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
.

3.1.1.
Actions by derivations

Let
(\mathfrak{f},m)\in\mathcal{C}
be an operadic Lie (respectively, an associative) algebra with bracket (respectively, multiplication)
m\colon\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
and let
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
denote a
\mathfrak{g}
-module structure on the underlying object.
We say that
\mathfrak{g}

acts by derivations
on
(\mathfrak{f},m)
if
m
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-module morphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Notice the presence of the braiding
c_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{f}}
in the definition of
\rho_{\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}
.

This generalizes the notion of an action by derivations for (operadic) Lie algebras in
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, where an
even
action
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
is by derivations if

\displaystyle\rho(x)[s,t]=[\rho(x)s,t]+(-1)^{|x||s|}[s,\rho(x)t].

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
an operadic Lie algebra with an action by derivations
\rho_{A}
on an associative algebra
(A,m)
in
\mathcal{C}
. Then
\mathfrak{g}
acts by derivations on the Lie algebra
(A,[\cdot,\cdot])
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
\rho_{A}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes m)=m\rho_{A\otimes A}
. Using this identity,

\displaystyle\rho_{A}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes[\cdot,\cdot])

\displaystyle=\rho_{A}\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes(m-mc_{A,A})\right)=\rho_{A}\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes m\right)-\rho_{A}\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes m\right)\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes c_{A,A}\right)

\displaystyle=m\rho_{A\otimes A}-m\rho_{A\otimes A}\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes c_{A,A}\right),

\displaystyle[\cdot,\cdot]\rho_{A\otimes A}

\displaystyle=m\rho_{A\otimes A}-mc_{A,A}\rho_{A\otimes A},

so it is enough to prove that
\rho_{A\otimes A}\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes c_{A,A}\right)=c_{A,A}\rho_{A\otimes A}
, or equivalently,

\displaystyle(\rho_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes c_{A,A}\right)+(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\rho_{A})(c_{\mathfrak{g},A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})\left(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes c_{A,A}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=c_{A,A}(\rho_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})+c_{A,A}(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\rho_{A})(c_{\mathfrak{g},A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A}).

The naturality of the braiding implies that

\displaystyle c_{A,A}(\rho_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\rho_{A})c_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes A,A},

\displaystyle c_{A,A}(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\rho_{A})

\displaystyle=(\rho_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})c_{A,\mathfrak{g}\otimes A}.

From these two identities and using that
c
satisfies the braid equation, we get

\displaystyle c_{A,A}(\rho_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})+c_{A,A}(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\rho_{A})(c_{\mathfrak{g},A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})

\displaystyle\quad=(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\rho_{A})c_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes A,A}+(\rho_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})c_{A,\mathfrak{g}\otimes A}(c_{\mathfrak{g},A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})

\displaystyle\quad=(\operatorname{id}_{A}\otimes\rho_{A})(c_{\mathfrak{g},A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes c_{A,A})+(\rho_{A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes c_{A,A})(c_{A,\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A})(c_{\mathfrak{g},A}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{A}),

and the claim follows.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Given
V\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{g})
, the
\mathfrak{g}
-action on
V
extends uniquely to an
\mathfrak{g}
-action by derivations on the tensor algebra
\operatorname{T}(V)\in\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
, both as an a associative and as a Lie algebra. Indeed the associative case is straightforward while the Lie case follows from Lemma
3.8
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Any action of
\mathfrak{g}
on an object
V
extends uniquely to an action of
\mathfrak{g}
on
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
by derivations.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.14
we may identify
\operatorname{FLie}(V)
with the Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{f}
of
\operatorname{T}(V)
generated by
V
. The
\mathfrak{g}
-action on
V
extends uniquely to an action on
\operatorname{T}(V)
by derivations, so we have to prove that
\mathfrak{f}
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-submodule.
By Remark
2.15
,
\mathfrak{f}=\oplus_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}
, so it suffices to check that each
\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-submodule. This last statement follows by induction on
n
, using that the action on
\operatorname{T}(V)
is by derivations.
∎

3.2.
Semidirect product of operadic Lie algebras

Now we are ready to introduce the notion of semidirect product.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{f}
be operadic Lie algebras in
\mathcal{C}
with an action
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
by derivations. The
Lie algebra semidirect product

\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
is the operadic Lie algebra with underlying space
\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
and Lie bracket
b
defined by the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g})\otimes(\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\,\oplus\,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\,\oplus\,\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\,\oplus\,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\mathfrak{f}}\oplus\rho\oplus(-\rho c_{\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}})\oplus b_{\mathfrak{g}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{f}}\sqcup\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{f}}\sqcup\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{f}})\oplus\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}}

Remark 3.12
.

Let
\iota_{1}:\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
,
\pi_{1}:\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{f}
,
\iota_{2}:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
,
\pi_{2}:\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
be the canonical maps.
Identifying
\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\,\oplus\,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\,\oplus\,\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\,\oplus\,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}
with
(\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g})\otimes(\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g})
via the maps
\iota_{i}\otimes\iota_{j}
,
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, the bracket of
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
is the map
b

such that

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2ptb\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{1}\circ b_{\mathfrak{f}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2ptb\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}}

\displaystyle=-\iota_{1}\circ\rho\circ c_{\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2ptb\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{1}\circ\rho,

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2ptb\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{2}\circ b_{\mathfrak{g}}.

In the case of operadic Lie algebras in
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
with an action by derivations
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
in
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
(i.e., an even map), we recover the usual definition of the semidirect product
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
:

\displaystyle\big[(t_{1},x_{1}),(t_{2},x_{2})\big]=\left[[t_{1},t_{2}]+\rho(x_{1})t_{2}-(-1)^{|t_{1}||x_{2}|}\rho(x_{2})t_{1},[x_{1},x_{2}]\right].

The next crucial example gives an element-free description of contragradient Lie superalgebras, and will serve as a guide throughout
Section

4
, where we construct contragredient Lie algebras in more abstract symmetric tensor categories. In particular, it is the main reason why we are interested in semidirect products of Lie algebras.

Example 3.13
.

Consider data as in
(D1)
–
(D4)
, and view
\mathfrak{h}
as a purely even abelian Lie algebra in
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
.
From these, we obtain an
\mathfrak{h}
-module
\rho\colon\mathfrak{h}\otimes V\to V
in
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
and a compatible map
d\colon V\otimes V^{*}\to\mathfrak{h}
as follows.

As an object,
V\in\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
has a basis
(e_{i})_{1\leq i\leq r}
according to the given parity vector; we obtain an action
\rho
on
V
by letting
\mathfrak{h}
act each on each
e_{i}
by the given character
\xi_{i}
. On the dual module
V^{*}
, with dual basis
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq r}
, the action of
\mathfrak{h}
is given by the characters
(-\xi_{i})
. Extend these to an action by derivations
\rho
of
\mathfrak{h}
on the free Lie superalgebra
\operatorname{FLie}(V\oplus V^{*})
.

In this case
\operatorname{FLie}(V\oplus V^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{h}
is the Lie superalgebra defined by the relations (
2.17
) except for
[e_{i},f_{j}]=\delta_{ij}h_{i}
. To realize the missing relation, let
d
be the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\colon V\otimes V^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h},}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}\otimes f_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{ij}h_{i}.}

Then the Lie superalgebra
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}(A,\mathbf{p})
associated to
(D1)
–
(D4)
is just the quotient of the semidirect product
\operatorname{FLie}(V\oplus V^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{h}
by the ideal generated by the image of

([\cdot,\cdot],-d)\colon V\otimes V^{*}\longrightarrow\operatorname{FLie}(V\oplus V^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{h},

where
[\cdot,\cdot]
denotes the restriction of the Lie bracket of
\operatorname{FLie}(V\oplus V^{*})
to
V\otimes V^{*}
. Notice that
d
is a map of
\mathfrak{h}
-modules, i.e.

\displaystyle[h,d(e_{i}\otimes f_{j})]

\displaystyle=0=d\left(h\cdot e_{i}\otimes f_{j}\right)+d\left(e_{i}\otimes h\cdot f_{j}\right),

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle\in\mathfrak{h},

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\leq i,j\leq r.

Moreover, this compatibility between the bracket in
\mathfrak{h}
and the map
d
is a necessary consequence of the Jacobi identity in
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}(A,\mathbf{p})
.

The semidirect product in
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})
satisfies the following expected property.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{f}
be operadic Lie algebras in
\mathcal{C}
with an action
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
by derivations.
Then
\iota_{1}\colon\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
,
\iota_{2}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
,
\pi_{2}\colon\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
are morphisms of operadic Lie algebras.
Thus the semidirect product
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
fits in a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\to\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}\to 0

of operadic Lie algebras in
\mathcal{C}
, which splits under
\iota_{2}
.∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{f}
be operadic Lie algebras with an action by derivations
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
in a symmetric tensor category. If
\mathfrak{g}
and
\mathfrak{f}
satisfy the
p
-Jacobi identity, then so does
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof.

We have to prove that
\beta^{\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}}
annihilates
E_{p}(\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})
. By
[
17
]
*Proposition 4.5,

\displaystyle E_{p}(\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})

\displaystyle=\ker\phi^{\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}}_{p}=\ker\phi^{\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}}_{p}=E_{p}(\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g})=E_{p}(\mathfrak{f})\oplus E_{p}(\mathfrak{g}).

On the other hand, by naturality of
\beta
, and since the canonical inclusions
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
are Lie algebra maps, we have
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\beta^{\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{f}}}=\beta^{\mathfrak{f}}
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\beta^{\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}}}=\beta^{\mathfrak{g}}
. By hypothesis,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\beta^{\mathfrak{f}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E_{p}(\mathfrak{f})}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\beta^{\mathfrak{g}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{E_{p}(\mathfrak{g})}}=0
, so
\beta^{\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}}
annihilates
E_{p}(\mathfrak{f})\oplus E_{p}(\mathfrak{g})=E_{p}(\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})
.
∎

Corollary 3.16
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a Frobenius exact pre-Tannakian category of moderate growth, then the subcategory
\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}})
of
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}})
is closed under semidirect products.

Proof.

Follows immediately from
Theorem

2.13
and
Lemma

3.15

∎

Remark 3.17
.

The semidirect product is universal. Indeed, we can imitate the element-free universal property for semidirect products of finite groups to describe the assignment
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{f},\rho)\mapsto(\mathfrak{g}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})
as a left adjoint.

Consider on one hand
\mathsf{OLieActions}(\mathcal{C})
, the category with objects
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{f},\rho)
, where
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
is an action of operadic Lie algebras by derivations, and arrows
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{f},\rho)\to(\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
given by pairs
(f_{1}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}^{\prime},f_{2}\colon\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
satisfying the obvious compatibility.

On the other hand, let
\mathsf{Arr}\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})
denote the category whose objects are the arrows in
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})
, and where morphisms are commutative diagrams.

We introduce the
pullback forgetful functor

\mathsf{Arr}\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{OLieActions}(\mathcal{C})
, which sends an arrow
\phi\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{f}
in
\mathsf{OLie}(\mathcal{C})
to the pullback of the adjoint action of
\mathfrak{f}
along
\phi
, that is,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{f}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{\mathfrak{f}}}

Then the assignment
(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{f},\rho)\mapsto(\mathfrak{g}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})
serves as a left adjoint for the pullback.

Remark 3.18
.

There is a close relation between the semidirect product introduced above and the smash product from
[
28
]
*Definition 5.30.
Namely, we can see
\mathfrak{f}
as a
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
-module due to Remark
3.5
, and then construct the smash product

\operatorname{T}(\mathfrak{f})\rtimes\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
. As the
\mathfrak{g}
-action is by derivations, the defining ideal of
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{f})
is
\mathfrak{g}
-stable (hence also
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
-stable), so we can quotient and obtain a smash product Hopf algebra
H=\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{f})\rtimes\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Assume further that
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{g}
are PBW, so we can view
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{g}
as subobjects of
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{f})
and
\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{g})
, respectively.
Since the commutator of
H
restricted to
\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}
coincides with that of
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
, we may identify
\mathfrak{f}\oplus\mathfrak{g}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathds{1}\oplus\mathds{1}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\subset H
. From here,
H\simeq\operatorname{U}(\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})
.

Next we describe the obstruction governing whether a pair morphisms from the factors can be extended to a morphisms from their semidirect product.

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{C}
be operadic Lie algebras. Assume
\mathfrak{g}
acts by derivations on
\mathfrak{f}
via
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
, and let
\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{h}
,
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
be Lie algebra morphisms. Set
\phi\coloneqq\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\sqcup\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
.
Then
\phi
is a Lie algebra morphism if and only if

(3.20)

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ\rho

\displaystyle=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}})\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{h}.

Proof.

Assume first that
\phi
is a Lie algebra map, i.e.
\phi\circ b=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi\otimes\phi)
. Then

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ\rho

\displaystyle=\phi\circ\iota_{1}\circ\rho=\phi\circ b\circ(\iota_{1}\otimes\iota_{2})=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi\otimes\phi)\circ(\iota_{1}\otimes\iota_{2})=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}),

so (
3.20
) holds.

Reciprocally, assume that (
3.20
) holds. The equality
\phi_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ b=b\circ(\phi\otimes\phi)

holds if and only if
\phi\circ b\circ(\iota_{i}\otimes\iota_{j})=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi\otimes\phi)\circ(\iota_{i}\otimes\iota_{j})
holds for all
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. The case

i=1
,
j=2
is exactly (
3.20
) while
i=2
,
j=1
follows by composing with
c_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{f}}
, and the cases

i=j=1
and
i=j=2
follow since
\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}
,
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}
are Lie algebra morphisms, respectively. Thus
\phi
is a Lie algebra morphism.
∎

3.2.1.
Modules over a semidirect product

Finally we construct modules over the semidirect product starting from an object which is simultaneously a module over both Lie algebras.

Proposition 3.21
.

Assume we have an action by derivations
\rho\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{f}
of operadic Lie algebras. Let
V\in\mathcal{C}
be simultaneously a
\mathfrak{g}
-module and an
\mathfrak{f}
-module, with actions
\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\otimes V\to V
,
\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes V\to V
. Set
\eta\coloneqq\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}\sqcup\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}\otimes V\to V

4
4

4

Here we identify
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}\otimes V=\mathfrak{f}\otimes V\oplus\mathfrak{g}\otimes V
as objects in
\mathcal{C}
.
.

Then
\eta
defines an
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
-module structure on
V
if and only if

(3.22)

\displaystyle\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V})

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\eta_{\mathfrak{f}})-\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{f}}\otimes\eta_{\mathfrak{g}})(c_{\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{f}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V}).

Proof.

If
\eta
gives an action, then we get (
3.22
) by restricting (
3.1
) to
\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\otimes V\subset(\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes 2}\otimes V
.

On the other hand, assume that
\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}
and
\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfy (
3.22
). We have to check that
\eta
makes the diagram (
3.1
) commutative, i.e, the two obvious maps
(\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g})^{\otimes 2}\otimes V\to V
are actually equal. It suffices to show so when restricted to
\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\otimes V
,
\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{f}\otimes V
,
\mathfrak{f}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\otimes V
, and
\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g}\otimes V
. The equality of these restrictions to the first and the fourth summands follow since
\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}
and
\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}
are actions. The second one is exactly (
3.22
), and the third one is just (
3.22
) pre-composed with
-c_{\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{V}
.
∎

A particularly relevant example of semidirect product can be obtained by taking
\mathfrak{f}=\operatorname{FLie}(W)
for a
\mathfrak{g}
-module
W
. We show next that it suffices to check the compatibilities in Propositions
3.19
and
3.21
just over
W
.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
W\in\mathcal{C}
be a
\mathfrak{g}
-module, and denote by
\rho
the corresponding action of
\mathfrak{g}
on
\mathfrak{f}=\operatorname{FLie}(W)
by derivations as in
Lemma

3.10
.

(i)

Let
\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{C}
,
\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{h}
,
\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
be as in
Proposition

3.19
.
Then
\phi\coloneqq\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\sqcup\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}:\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{h}
. is a Lie algebra map if and only if (
3.20
) holds on
\mathfrak{g}\otimes W
.

(ii)

Let
V\in\mathcal{C}
,
\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\otimes V\to V
,
\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{g}\otimes V\to V
be as in
Proposition

3.21
.
Then
V
is an
\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}
-module via
\eta\coloneqq\eta_{\mathfrak{f}}\sqcup\eta_{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\rtimes_{\rho}\mathfrak{g}\otimes V\to V
if and only if (
3.22
) holds on
\mathfrak{g}\otimes W\otimes V
.

Proof.

As in Remark
2.15
, let
\varphi_{1}:W\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{f}
be the inclusion and
\varphi_{n+1}\coloneq{\left.\kern-1.2ptb_{\mathfrak{f}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes W}}
.

The forward implication in
(i)
follows directly from
Proposition

3.19
. Conversely, assume that (
3.20
) holds on
\mathfrak{g}\otimes W
. As
\mathfrak{f}=\oplus_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}
, we have to check that

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ\rho\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\varphi_{n})

\displaystyle=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\varphi_{n})

\displaystyle\text{for all }n\in\mathbb{N}.

The proof is by induction on
n
; the case
n=1
is exactly our assumption. Now assume that the equality above holds for
n
. Using that
\rho
acts by derivations,
\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}
is a Lie algebra map, inductive hypothesis and the Jacobi identity

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ

\displaystyle\rho\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\varphi_{n+1})=\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ\rho\circ{\left.\kern-1.2ptb_{\mathfrak{f}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes W}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ b_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ{\left.\kern-1.2pt\big(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W}+(\operatorname{id}\otimes\rho)(c_{\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W})\big)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes W}}

\displaystyle=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ{\left.\kern-1.2pt\big(\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ\rho\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}+(\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ\rho)(c_{\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W})\big)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes W}}

\displaystyle=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ{\left.\kern-1.2pt\big(b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}})\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}+(\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\otimes b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}))(c_{\mathfrak{g},\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{W})\big)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes W}}

\displaystyle=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes b_{\mathfrak{h}})\circ{\left.\kern-1.2pt\big(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\big)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes W}}

\displaystyle=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ{\left.\kern-1.2pt\big(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\circ b_{\mathfrak{h}}\big)\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathfrak{g}\otimes\operatorname{im}\varphi_{n}\otimes W}}=b_{\mathfrak{h}}\circ(\phi_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\phi_{\mathfrak{f}})\circ(\operatorname{id}_{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\varphi_{n+1}),

thus the inductive step follows.

The proof of
(ii)
follows similarly.
∎

4.
Contragredient Lie algebras

This Section contains the main constructions and results of this work. The goal is to construct a notion of contragredient Lie algebras in a symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
. The input necessary for the construction will be called contragredient data. Actually, we will introduce three hierarchically ordered notions of such data, starting with a rather relaxed one that will progressively get richer, thus allowing proofs fore deeper structural results.

4.1.
Contragredient data

We begin with the following data:

Definition 4.1
.

Consider a Lie algebra
(\mathtt{X},\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{X}})
in a strict symmetric tensor category
\mathcal{C}
. A
contragredient datum
over
\mathtt{X}
is a pair
(\rho,\mathtt{d})
where:

(D1)

\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V}
is an
\mathtt{X}
-module in
\mathcal{C}
, and

(D2)

\mathtt{d}\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{X}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
,

which are compatible in the following sense:

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{X}}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}}\otimes\mathtt{d})=\mathtt{d}(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}})+\mathtt{d}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}}\otimes\rho^{\vee})(c_{\mathtt{X},\mathtt{V}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}).

Here, as in (
3.4
),
\rho^{\vee}\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{V}^{*}
is the dual module defined by the composition

(4.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\vee}\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}^{*},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathtt{X},\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\mathsf{coev}_{\mathtt{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes(-\rho)\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{ev}_{\mathtt{V}}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}

and (
4.2
) means that
\mathtt{d}
is a map of
\mathtt{X}
-modules.

We denote by
\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
the set of pairs
(\rho,\mathtt{d})
as above. If we want to emphasize
\mathtt{V}
, we say that
(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is an
\mathtt{X}
-contragredient structure on
\mathtt{V}
.

Remark 4.4
.

Let
(\rho,\mathtt{d})\in\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
. As
\mathcal{C}
is symmetric,
\mathtt{V}
has a natural structure of dual object to
\mathtt{V}^{*}
and
(\rho^{\vee})^{\vee}
identifies with
\rho
. That is, there exists a natural isomorphism
\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}}\colon\mathtt{V}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathtt{V}^{**}
which is a map of
\mathtt{X}
-modules. Define
\mathtt{d}^{\vee}\colon\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{**}\to\mathtt{X}
by

(4.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{d}^{\vee}\colon\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{**}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{X}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\mathtt{d}}

Then one can see that the pair
(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})
satisfies the compatibility (
4.2
), i.e.
(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})\in\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
.

Given a contragredient datum, and employing the usual diagrammatic calculus in strict symmetric tensor categories
5
5

5

We read diagrams from top to bottom.
, we introduce the following:

Notation 4.6
.

(1)

The morphisms
\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{X}}
,
\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}}
,
\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}
,
\rho
, and
\mathtt{d}
are denoted by the diagrams

\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{X}}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 17.67181pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-17.67181pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-17.67181pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to35.34pt{\vbox to35.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 17.67181pt\lower-34.74364pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

(3)

By
Lemma

3.10
,
\rho
and
\rho^{\vee}
induce an action by derivations

\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\longrightarrow\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*}).

4.2.
The auxiliary operadic Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})

Definition 4.8
.

Let
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as in
Definition

4.1
. We define the operadic Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
as the quotient of
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
by the ideal generated by the image of

(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}},-\mathtt{d})\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\longrightarrow\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X},

where
\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}
denotes the restriction of the Lie bracket
\mathtt{b}
of
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})
to
\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}
.

Remark 4.9
.

•

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is such that
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}})
is closed under semidirect products. This holds for example if
\mathcal{C}
is a Frobenius exact pre-Tannakian category of moderate growth, thanks to
Corollary

3.16
. Since
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is a quotient of
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
, it follows from
[
17
]
*Lemma 7.6 that
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is a Lie algebra in
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
, rather than an operadic one.

•

There are natural maps
\mathtt{X},\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
; we will prove these are injective in
Theorem

4.18
.

•

The map
\mathtt{V}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
extends to a Lie algebra map
\iota_{+}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
.
6
6

6

This is not a direct consequence of the definition of
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})
because
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is an operadic Lie algebra.
Indeed, one constructs
\iota_{+}
as the composition

\iota_{+}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\to\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d}).

Similarly,
\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
extends to a Lie algebra map
\iota_{-}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
.

•

We denote by
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
the subalgebras of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
generated by
\iota_{+}(\mathtt{V})
and
\iota_{-}(\mathtt{V}^{*})
, resp.

•

There is a
\mathbb{Z}
-grading on the ind-Lie algebra
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
determined by

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{V}^{*}

\displaystyle=-1,

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{X}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\deg\mathtt{V}

\displaystyle=1.

Since the defining ideal of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is homogeneous, we get a grading

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}(\rho,\mathtt{d}).

We obtain first a sort of Cartan involution for the algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
, which relies on the natural built-in duality of
\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
described in
Remark

4.4
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
(\rho,\mathtt{d})\in\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
. There exists a unique operadic Lie algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}\to\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{**})\rtimes_{\rho^{\vee}}\mathtt{X}

with

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widehat{\omega}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widehat{\omega}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}}}=\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widehat{\omega}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}.

Moreover,
\widehat{\omega}
descends to the quotient and induces an operadic Lie algebras isomorphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}\colon\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee}).

Proof.

First, we prove the existence of
\widehat{\omega}
.
We apply
Lemma

3.23
for
W=\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*}
,
\mathfrak{g}=\mathtt{X}
,
\mathfrak{h}=\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{**})\rtimes_{\rho^{\vee}}\mathtt{X}
. Here
\mathfrak{f}=\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})
, we take
\phi_{\mathfrak{f}}\colon\mathfrak{f}\to\mathfrak{h}
as the unique Lie algebra map whose restriction to
\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*}
is
\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}}\sqcup\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}
, and
\phi_{\mathtt{X}}:\mathtt{X}\to\mathfrak{h}
is the inclusion. Now the compatibility (
3.20
) holds trivially on
\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}
, and holds on
\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}
since
\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}}
is a map of
\mathtt{X}
-modules. Thus there exists a Lie algebra map
\widehat{\omega}
as stated.

By the same argument, there is a map
\varpi\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{**})\rtimes_{\rho^{\vee}}\mathtt{X}\to\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
such that

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\varpi\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}^{**}}}=\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}}^{-1}
,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\varpi\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}
,
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\varpi\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}}
. Since
\mathtt{V}^{*}
,
\mathtt{X}
and
\mathtt{V}
generate
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
, it is clear that
\varpi
is the inverse of
\widehat{\omega}
, which is thus an isomorphism.

The next step is to prove that
\widehat{\omega}
factors through the quotient and hence yields the desired Lie algebra map
\widetilde{\omega}
. Let
\pi\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{**})\rtimes_{\rho^{\vee}}\mathtt{X}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})
be the projection. We show that
\pi\widehat{\omega}
annihilates the image of
(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}},-\mathtt{d})
.
By definition of
\widehat{\omega}
and since the bracket is antisymmetric, we have

\widehat{\omega}(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}},0)=(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}^{**}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}},0)(\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}})=(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{**}},0)(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}})c_{\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}}.

From here, and using the definition of
\pi
and
\mathtt{d}^{\vee}
we obtain

\displaystyle\pi\widehat{\omega}(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}},0)

\displaystyle=-\pi(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{**}},0)(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}})c_{\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}}=-\pi(0,\mathtt{d}^{\vee})(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}})c_{\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}}=\pi\widehat{\omega}(0,\mathtt{d});

thus
\pi\widehat{\omega}(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}},-\mathtt{d})=0
, as desired, which implies the existence of
\widetilde{\omega}
.

Similarly, the inverse
\varpi
for
\widehat{\omega}
introduced above descends to the quotient thus producing an inverse for
\widetilde{\omega}
.
∎

Next we develop some structural results for
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
. Namely, we want to obtain a triangular decomposition, prove that the subalgebras
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
from
Remark

4.9
are the free Lie algebras on
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
, respectively, and finally verify that there exists a maximal ideal trivially intersecting
\mathtt{X}
. Essentially, we follow the strategy from
[
20
]
*Theorem 1.2. In particular, our proofs of existence and several structural properties of contragredient Lie algebras will rely heavily on a family of modules.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
(\rho,\mathtt{d})\in\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
and
(\mathtt{W},\lambda)\in\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathtt{X})
. Consider the following maps:

(1)

\eta_{\mathtt{X}}\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
is the tensor product of the
\mathtt{X}
-modules
(\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V}),\rho)
(obtained from
(\mathtt{V},\rho)
as in
Remark

3.9
) and
(\mathtt{W},\lambda)
.

(2)

\eta_{+}\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
is the multiplication on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})
tensored with
\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{W}}
.

(3)

\eta_{-}\colon\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
is defined recursively
\eta_{-}^{n}\colon\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\mathtt{V}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\mathtt{W}
by

\displaystyle\eta_{-}^{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\eta_{-}^{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\mathtt{d}\circ c_{\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{V}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\mathtt{W}})+(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}}\otimes\eta_{-}^{n-1})(c_{\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{V}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes(n-1)}\otimes\mathtt{W}})\quad n\geq 1.

Then these three maps extend uniquely to an action

\eta_{\rtimes}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}.

Moreover,
\eta_{\rtimes}
descends to the quotient thus producing an action of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

Proof.

Claim 1
.

There is a unique action
\eta_{\operatorname{F}}
of
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})
on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
extending
\eta_{+}
and
\eta_{-}
.

Indeed, by
Remark

3.7
, the map
(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
defined as
\eta_{+}
on
\mathtt{V}
and as
\eta_{-}
on
\mathtt{V}^{*}
extends uniquely to an action
\eta_{\operatorname{F}}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

Claim 2
.

The actions
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}
from (
(1)
) and
\eta_{\operatorname{F}}
from
1
extend uniquely to an action
\eta_{\rtimes}
of
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

By
Proposition

3.21
, we just need to check that
\eta_{\operatorname{F}}
and
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}
satisfy the compatibility (
3.22
). That is, we must verify the following identity on
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}\big(\mathtt{X}\otimes\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W},\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\big)
:

(4.12)

\displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{F}}(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}})=\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}}\otimes\eta_{\operatorname{F}})-\eta_{\operatorname{F}}(\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})}\otimes\eta_{\mathtt{X}})(c_{\mathtt{X},\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}}).

By
Lemma

3.23

(ii)
, it suffices to check this equality on
\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
and
\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

To verify the identity on the first term, we restrict to
\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}
, where it becomes

which follows directly since
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}
is defined by extending
\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V}
by derivations.

Now we verify recursively on
n\geq 0
that (
4.12
) holds in
\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

•

For
n=0
, the three terms of (
4.12
) vanish. Indeed,
\eta_{-}(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes 0}\otimes\mathtt{W}})=\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}}\otimes\eta_{-})=0
by definition of
\eta_{-}
.
Similarly, the remaining term is
0
since
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes 0}\otimes\mathtt{W})\subseteq\mathtt{V}^{\otimes 0}\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

•

n=1
to illustrate the diagrammatic computations involved in the proof of the inductive step. Here (
4.12
) becomes
