Now we compute the sum
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta^{n}_{-})-\eta_{-}^{n}(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta_{\mathtt{X}})(c\otimes\operatorname{id})
. Notice that the second summands in each of these two terms cancel out mutually. Also, as illustrated in the case
n=1
, applying (
4.7
) to the module
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}
, the first summand of
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta^{n}_{-})
together with the first and third summands of
-\eta_{-}^{n}(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta_{\mathtt{X}})(c\otimes\operatorname{id})
add up exactly to the first summand of
\eta_{-}^{n}
. Lastly, by induction, the third summand of
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta^{n}_{-})
together with the fourth summand of
-\eta_{-}^{n}(\operatorname{id}\otimes\eta_{\mathtt{X}})(c\otimes\operatorname{id})
give the second summand of
\eta_{-}^{n}
. Thus (
4.12
) holds also over
\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

This finishes the proof of
2
.

Claim 3
.

The map
\eta_{\rtimes}
from
2
factors through the quotient, thus defining an action of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
.

Indeed, by definition of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\mathtt{X},\mathtt{V})
, we just need to verify that

\eta_{\rtimes}\big(\operatorname{im}(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}},-\mathtt{d})\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\big)=0.

To do so, it is enough to verify that, for each
n\geq 0
, the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{d}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\operatorname{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathtt{X}}}

The proof uses that
\eta_{\operatorname{F}}
gives a module structure together with the recursive definition of
\eta_{-}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\eta_{\operatorname{F}}

\displaystyle\big(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}}\big)=\eta_{+}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}}\otimes\eta_{-}^{n})-\eta_{-}^{n+1}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\eta_{+})(c_{\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{W}})

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}}\otimes\eta_{-}^{n})-\big(-\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\mathtt{d}\circ c_{\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{V}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}})+(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}}\otimes\eta_{-}^{n})(c_{\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{V}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}})\big)(c_{\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}})

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathtt{X}}\big(\mathtt{d}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{\otimes n}\otimes\mathtt{W}}\big),

and the statement follows.
∎

In order to obtain a triangular decomposition as in the classical case, we need to impose a restriction on the Lie algebra
\mathtt{X}
. As will become clear later, this algebra plays a role similar to that of a Cartan subalgebra. Thus, for the examples of interest at the moment, it will make no harm to assume that it belong to some well-behaved class of Lie algebras.

Definition 4.13
.

We say that
\mathtt{X}\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
has
enough modules
if for every simple subobject
S\subseteq\mathtt{X}
there exists an
\mathtt{X}
-module
\lambda\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\mathtt{W}
such that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\lambda\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\otimes\mathtt{W}}}\neq 0
.

Example 4.14
.

In the classical case over
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, see
Example

3.13
, one takes
\mathtt{X}=\mathfrak{h}
, a purely even abelian Lie algebra, which clearly has enough modules since each element of
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
defines one.

Example 4.15
.

If
\mathtt{X}
is a simple Lie algebra (i.e., non-abelian and contains no proper ideals), then it has enough modules. In fact, for any simple object
S\subseteq\mathtt{X}
the adjoint action
\operatorname{ad}\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{X}\to\mathtt{X}
satisfies
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\operatorname{ad}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}\otimes S}}\neq 0
.

Example 4.16
.

More interesting examples can be constructed over
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
for
p\geq 5
. Namely, take a simple
\mathtt{L}_{i}
in
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
with
i\neq 1,p-1
, and consider the general Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathtt{L}_{i})
, see
Remark

3.2
. By
[
29
]
*Proposition 3.8, Theorem 4.4, we have a Lie algebra decomposition
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathtt{L}_{i})=\mathds{1}\oplus\mathfrak{sl}(\mathtt{L}_{i})
where
\mathds{1}
is central and
\mathfrak{sl}(\mathtt{L}_{i})
is a simple Lie algebra. Thus, by the previous examples,
\mathfrak{gl}(\mathtt{L}_{i})
has enough modules.

Example 4.17
.

Since
\mathsf{Ver}_{p}
contains
\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, we can merge
Examples

4.14
and
4.16
and take the direct sum Lie algebra
\mathtt{X}=\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{g}(\mathtt{L}_{i})\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathsf{Ver}_{p})
, which also has enough modules.

Now we obtain some fundamental structural results for the auxiliary algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
. Recall the subalgebras
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{\pm}\subset\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
and the grading
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
from
Remark

4.9
.

Theorem 4.18
.

Let
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as in
Definition

4.1
. Assume that
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules in the sense of
Definition

4.13
. Then, for the operadic Lie algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\coloneq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
, the following hold:

(a)

The natural map
\iota_{0}\colon\mathtt{X}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
is an inclusion.

(b)

The Lie subalgebras
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
are free on
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
, respectively. Moreover, the natural maps
\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
induce Lie algebras isomorphisms
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\cong\mathtt{n}_{+}
and
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})\cong\mathtt{n}_{-}
.
7
7

7

For this part we do not need to assume that
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules.

(c)

We have
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}=\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
as objects of
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
. Moreover,

(4.19)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k<0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k},

\displaystyle\mathtt{X}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{0},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k>0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}.

(d)

Among all
\mathbb{Z}
-homogeneous ideals of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
trivially intersecting
\mathtt{X}
, there exists a unique maximal one
\mathtt{m}
. Moreover,
\mathtt{m}=\mathtt{m}_{+}\oplus\mathtt{m}_{-}
, where
\mathtt{m}_{\pm}=\mathtt{m}\cap\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{\pm}
.

Proof.

We begin with
(a)
. Assume for a contradiction that
\ker\iota_{0}\neq 0
, and take a simple subobject
S
in this kernel. Since
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules, there is some module
\lambda\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\mathtt{W}
such that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\lambda\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\otimes\mathtt{W}}}\neq 0
. By
Lemma

4.11
this
\lambda
induces an action
\eta\colon\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
. By construction of
\eta
, if
u\colon\mathds{1}\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})
denotes the unit map, we get a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\otimes\mathtt{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{0}\otimes u\otimes\mathtt{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u\otimes\lambda_{|S\otimes\mathtt{W}}}

The up-right-down path of this diagram is zero, but the bottom arrow is not. Thus
\iota_{0}
is injective.

For
(b)
, we show that the Lie algebra map
\iota_{+}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
extending
\mathtt{V}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
(see
Remark

4.9
) is injective. Denote by
\nu_{\mathds{1}}
the
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
-action on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})
obtained by taking the trivial
\mathtt{X}
-module
\mathtt{W}=\mathds{1}
in Lemma
4.11
. The pull back of
\nu_{\mathds{1}}
along
\iota_{+}
produces an
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})
-action on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})
, which happens to coincide with the adjoint action (because they agree on
\mathtt{V}
, which generates). Thus the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\mathds{1}}}

is just the inclusion
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\hookrightarrow\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})
. Thence
\iota_{+}
is injective. Since the image of
\iota_{+}
is clearly
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
, it induces a canonical isomorphism
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\cong\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
.

The same argument applied to
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})
shows that the subalgebra generated by
\mathtt{V}^{*}
(the
positive part
of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})
) is free on
\mathtt{V}^{*}
. The isomorphism
\widetilde{\omega}\colon\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})
from
Lemma

4.10
maps
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
to the subalgebra generated by
\mathtt{V}^{*}
in
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})
, so
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
is free on
\mathtt{V}^{*}
. Moreover, by naturality, the isomorphism
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})\cong\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
is provided by the natural map
\iota_{-}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
from
Remark

4.9
.

Next we deal with
(c)
. We have to prove that the map

(4.20)

\displaystyle\iota\coloneq\iota_{-}\sqcup\iota_{0}\sqcup\iota_{+}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}

is an isomorphism of ind-objects. We show that it is injective and surjective.

Claim 1
.

The map
\iota
from (
4.20
) is injective.

Indeed, take
N_{-}\oplus N_{0}\oplus N_{+}\subseteq\ker\iota
, where
N_{-}\subseteq\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})
,
N_{0}\subseteq\mathtt{X}
and
N_{+}\subseteq\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})
. As before, consider the action
\nu_{\mathds{1}}\colon\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\to\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})
, and let
\mathtt{F}
denote the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{F}\colon\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}^{*})\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\mathds{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\mathds{1}}}

By construction of
\nu_{\mathds{1}}
(see
Lemma

4.11
), we know that, when restricted to
\mathds{1}\subset\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})
, the action of
\mathtt{V}^{*}
is trivial while
\mathtt{V}
acts by multiplication; thus

0=\mathtt{F}(N_{-}\oplus N_{0}\oplus N_{+})=\nu_{\mathds{1}}(\iota_{0}(N_{0})\otimes\mathds{1})\oplus\iota_{+}(N_{+}).

So we get
N_{+}=0
. Consider now an arbitrary
\mathtt{X}
-module
(\mathtt{W},\lambda)
, and let
\eta
denote the associated
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
-action on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
provided by
Lemma

4.11
. Then, by construction,

\displaystyle\eta\big(\iota(N_{-}\oplus N_{0})\otimes\mathtt{V}^{0}\otimes\mathtt{W}\big)=\eta_{\mathtt{X}}(\iota_{0}(N_{0})\otimes\mathds{1}\otimes\mathtt{W})=\mathds{1}\otimes\lambda(N_{0}\otimes\mathtt{W}).

As
\iota(N_{-}\oplus N_{0})=0
, we get
\lambda(N_{0}\otimes\mathtt{W})=0
for an arbitrary
\mathtt{X}
-module
(\lambda,\mathtt{W})
. But we are assuming that
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules, so
N_{0}=0
. Finally
0=\iota(N_{-})=\iota_{-}(N_{-})
, so
N_{-}=0
. This finishes the proof of
1
.

Claim 2
.

The image of the map
\iota
from (
4.20
) is a Lie subalgebra. Thus
\iota
is surjective.

Let
\mathtt{b}\colon\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\otimes\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
denote the Lie bracket. We show that, when restricted to a tensor product of any two terms among
\operatorname{im}\iota_{-}
,
\operatorname{im}\iota_{0}
, and
\operatorname{im}\iota_{+}
, the image of
\mathtt{b}
lies on
\operatorname{im}\iota
.

\diamond

For any
\star\in\{-,0,+\}
, since
\iota_{\star}
is a Lie algebra map, we have
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{\star}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{\star})\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota_{\star}
.

\diamond

Also,
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{0}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{\pm})\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota_{\pm}
, since
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
is a quotient of
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
.

\diamond

The only delicate case is
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+})\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota
. We know from part
(b)
that
\iota_{\pm}
canonically identify
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{\pm}
with the corresponding free Lie algebras, thus we get decompositions
\operatorname{im}\iota_{\pm}=\oplus_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{im}\iota_{\pm,n}
. More precisely, we get such decomposition where

\displaystyle\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,1}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{V}^{*},

\displaystyle\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,1}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{V},

\displaystyle\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n+1}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n}).

We check recursively on
m,n\geq 1
that
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,m}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n})\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota
. For
m=n=1
,

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,1}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,1})

\displaystyle=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V})=\mathtt{d}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V})\subseteq\mathtt{X}\subset\operatorname{im}\iota.

Then, using the Jacobi identity we check that

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,n+1}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,1})

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,n},

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,1}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n+1})

\displaystyle\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n},

\displaystyle\text{for all }n\geq 0.

A similar argument shows the general case. Indeed, assume that
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,r}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,s})\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota
when
r+s\leq k
and fix
m,n\geq 1
such that
m+n=k
. Using the Jacobi identity,

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\iota_{-,m+1}

\displaystyle\otimes\iota_{+,n})=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n}})\big(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\iota_{-,m}\otimes\iota_{+,n}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\mathtt{b})\big(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\iota_{-,m}\otimes\iota_{+,n}\big)+\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\mathtt{b})(c\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n}})\big(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}\otimes\iota_{-,m}\otimes\iota_{+,n}\big).

From (
4.21
) and the inductive hypothesis we conclude that
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,m+1}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n})\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota
. Analogously,
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota_{-,m}\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota_{+,n+1})\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota
.

Thus,
\mathtt{b}(\operatorname{im}\iota\otimes\operatorname{im}\iota)\subseteq\operatorname{im}\iota
, i.e.
\operatorname{im}\iota
is a subalgebra. Since
\mathtt{V}
,
\mathtt{V}^{*}
and
\mathtt{X}
are contained in
\operatorname{im}\iota
and they generate
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
as Lie algebra, we conclude that
\operatorname{im}\iota=\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
, which proves
2
.

To verify (
4.19
), note that the inclusions
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}\subseteq\oplus_{k<0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
and
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}\subseteq\oplus_{k>0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
follow since
\mathtt{V}^{*}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{-1}
,
\mathtt{V}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{1}
and both
\oplus_{k<0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
and
\oplus_{k>0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
are Lie subalgebras of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
, while
\mathtt{X}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{0}
by definition.
Thus (
4.19
) holds since we have proved that
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}=\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
.

Finally we prove
(d)
. Let
\mathtt{I}
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideal such that
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{X}=0
. Thus
\mathtt{I}=\oplus_{k\neq 0}\mathtt{I}_{k}
, where
\mathtt{I}_{k}=\mathtt{I}\cap\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
.
Take
\mathtt{m}
as the sum of all
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideals such that
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{X}=0
. Then
\mathtt{m}=\oplus_{k\neq 0}\mathtt{m}_{k}
, where
\mathtt{m}_{k}=\mathtt{m}\cap\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
is the sum of the degree
k
components of these
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideals, thus
\mathtt{m}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideal such that
\mathtt{m}\cap\mathtt{X}=0
, and
\mathtt{m}=\mathtt{m}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{m}_{+}
.
∎

Remark 4.22
.

By parts
(a)
and
(b)
, if
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules, then the canonical maps
\mathtt{X},\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
are inclusions. As a consequence of
Lemma

4.10
, the Cartan involution

(4.23)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}\colon\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})\to\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})

satisfies

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\omega}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\omega}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}}}=\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\omega}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}.

Similarly, by
Lemma

4.11
, each
\mathtt{X}
-module
\lambda\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{W}\to\mathtt{W}
induces a
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
-action
\eta
on
\operatorname{T}(\mathtt{V})\otimes\mathtt{W}
whose restrictions
\eta_{\mathtt{X}}
,
\eta_{+}
and
\eta_{-}
to
\mathtt{X}
,
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
are given by
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
, respectively.

Example 4.24
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, take a classical contragredient datum
(A,\mathbf{p})
as in
(D1)
–
(D4)
. As explained in
Example

3.13
, from this we extract a contragredient datum
(\rho,d)\in\operatorname{D}(\mathfrak{h})
, and we have
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}(A,\mathbf{p})=\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,d)
by definition.

4.3.
Definition and first properties of the contragredient Lie algebra
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})

Let us start with a somewhat pathological case.

Example 4.25
.

Assume
\mathtt{X}\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
has enough modules, and take any module
\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V}
. Then the zero map
0\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{X}
obviously satisfies (
4.2
) thus
(\rho,0)
constitutes a contragredient datum. In this case,
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
commute in the auxiliary algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,0)
, and we have a decomposition
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,0)=\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
where
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{+}
and
\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{-}
are actually ideals. Hence the quotient
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,0)/\mathtt{m}
is just
\mathtt{X}
.

The previous example shows that, even though one can run through the construction of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})/\mathtt{m}
with no assumption on the map
\mathtt{d}
apart from the compatibility (
4.2
), it is not reasonable to expect nice properties on the algebra
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})/\mathtt{m}
at this level of generality. To avoid such undesired behaviors, we impose a first restriction on the map
\mathtt{d}
.

Definition 4.26
.

Fix a Lie algebra
\mathtt{X}
, and take
(\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V},\mathtt{d}\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{X})\in\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
as in
Definition

4.1
. We say that the datum
(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is
reduced
if for any simple subobjects
S\subseteq\mathtt{V}
and
T\subseteq\mathtt{V}^{*}
, we have
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{d}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{S\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}}\neq 0
and
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{d}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}\otimes T}}\neq 0
.

We denote the set of reduced data over
\mathtt{X}
by
\operatorname{RD}(\mathtt{X})
.

Example 4.27
.

In the classical case over
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, see
Examples

4.24
and
3.13
, we extract
d\colon V\otimes V^{*}\to\mathfrak{h}
given by
e_{i}\otimes f_{j}\mapsto\delta_{ij}h_{i}
, which is clearly reduced.

Our next result shows how reducibility of
\mathtt{d}
replicates on
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
.

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as in
Definition

4.26
and assume
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules. If
\mathtt{I}
is an ideal of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
with
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{X}=0
, then also
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{V}=0
and
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{V}^{*}=0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathtt{b}
denote the bracket of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
. Assume for a contradiction that
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{V}\neq 0
, and take a simple
S\subseteq\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{V}
. Since
\mathtt{I}
is an ideal, we have
\mathtt{b}(S\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})\subseteq\mathtt{I}
, and by definition
\mathtt{d}
takes values on
\mathtt{X}
. Thus using that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{b}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}}
coincides with
\mathtt{d}
, we get

0\neq\mathtt{d}(S\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})=\mathtt{b}(S\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})\subseteq\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{X},

a contradiction. Thus
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{V}=0
, and similarly
\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{V}^{*}=0
.
∎

Remark 4.29
.

Consider
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as above. Then the Lie bracket of
\operatorname{F}\coloneq\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathtt{X}
clearly satisfies
\mathtt{b}_{\operatorname{F}}(\operatorname{F}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})=\operatorname{F}_{k-1}
for each integer
k
. Thus, if
\mathtt{q}
is a quotient of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
by a
\mathbb{Z}
-homogeneous ideal which intersects
\mathtt{X}
trivially, then we also have
\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{q}}(\mathtt{q}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})=\mathtt{q}_{k-1}
.

Now we are ready to introduce the main object of interest.

Definition 4.30
.

Fix
\mathtt{X}\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
with enough modules and take a reduced datum
(\rho,\mathtt{d})
over
\mathtt{X}
in the sense of
Definition

4.26
. The
contragredient Lie algebra
associated to the triple
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
is the operadic ind-Lie algebra
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})/\mathtt{m}
, where
\mathtt{m}
is the maximal ideal from
Theorem

4.18

(d)
.

All the observations from
Remark

4.9
factor through:

Remark 4.31
.

•

If
\mathcal{C}
is a Frobenius exact pre-Tannakian category of moderate growth, then
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is a Lie algebra in
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
, rather than an operadic one.

•

By definition of
\mathtt{m}
, and thanks to
Lemma

4.28
, the inclusions
\mathtt{X},\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
from
Theorem

4.18

(a)
,
(b)
induce inclusions
\mathtt{X},\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V}^{*}\hookrightarrow\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
.

•

By
Theorem

4.18

(d)
, the defining ideal of
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is homogeneous, which gives a grading

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathtt{g}_{k}(\rho,\mathtt{d}),

where
\mathtt{g}_{k}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
is the image of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
.

•

We denote by
\mathtt{n}_{+}
and
\mathtt{n}_{-}
the subalgebras of
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
generated by
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
, respectively.

•

The subset
\operatorname{RD}(\mathtt{X})\subseteq\operatorname{D}(\mathtt{X})
is closed under the duality introduced in
Remark

4.4
.

From
Theorem

4.18
we obtain the first structural results for contragredient Lie algebras.

Theorem 4.33
.

Let
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as in
Definition

4.30
, and assume that
\mathtt{X}
has enough modules. Then, for the operadic Lie algebra
\mathtt{g}\coloneq\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
, the following hold:

(a)

There exists a unique operadic Lie algebra isomorphism

(4.34)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\colon\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})\to\mathtt{g}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})

such that

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}}}=\vartheta_{\mathtt{V}},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\omega\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}}.

(b)

We have
\mathtt{g}=\mathtt{n}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\mathtt{n}_{+}
as objects in
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
. Moreover,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\mathtt{n}_{-}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k<0}\mathtt{g}_{k},

\displaystyle\mathtt{X}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{g}_{0},

\displaystyle\mathtt{n}_{+}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{k>0}\mathtt{g}_{k}.

(c)

If
Y
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-homogeneous subobject of
\mathtt{n}_{+}
(respectively,
\mathtt{n}_{-}
) such that
\mathtt{b}(Y\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})=0
(respectively,
\mathtt{b}(Y\otimes\mathtt{V})=0
), then
Y=0
.

Proof.

For
(a)
we check that the isomorphism
\widetilde{\omega}
from
Lemma

4.10
satisfies
\widetilde{\omega}(\mathtt{m})=\mathtt{m}^{\vee}
, where
\mathtt{m}^{\vee}
is the maximal
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideal of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})
among those that meet
\mathtt{X}
trivially. Notice that
\widetilde{\omega}(\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k})=\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho^{\vee},\mathtt{d}^{\vee})_{-k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, so
\widetilde{\omega}(\mathtt{m})
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideal, and
\widetilde{\omega}(\mathtt{m})\cap\mathtt{X}=0
since
\mathtt{X}=\widetilde{\omega}(\mathtt{X})
. Thus
\widetilde{\omega}(\mathtt{m})\subseteq\mathtt{m}^{\vee}
by maximality of
\mathtt{m}^{\vee}
. Analogously,
\widetilde{\omega}^{-1}(\mathtt{m}^{\vee})\subseteq\mathtt{m}^{\vee}
, so
\widetilde{\omega}(\mathtt{m})=\mathtt{m}^{\vee}
.

Now
(b)
follows from the definition of the grading (
4.32
), just by applying the canonical projection
\pi\colon\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathtt{g}
to the correspondent decompositions (
4.19
) for
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{n}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\pi(\widetilde{\mathtt{n}}_{\pm})=\pi\left(\oplus_{\pm k>0}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}\right)=\oplus_{\pm k>0}\mathtt{g}_{k},

\displaystyle\mathtt{X}

\displaystyle=\pi(\mathtt{X})=\pi\left(\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{0}\right)=\mathtt{g}_{0}.

Finally we prove
(c)
, for which suffices to deal with the case
Y\subseteq\mathtt{n}_{+}
since the other one follows by applying
\omega
. Set
n\in\mathbb{N}
as the degree of
Y
, and

\displaystyle\mathtt{I}

\displaystyle=Y+\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k}\otimes Y)\right).

We claim that
\mathtt{I}
is an ideal. As
\mathtt{g}
is generated by
\mathtt{V}
,
\mathtt{X}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
, it suffices to check that

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{I})

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathtt{I},

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{I})

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathtt{I},

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{I})

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathtt{I}.

The first inclusion follows since
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}\otimes Y)=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{1}\otimes Y)
is one of the summands defining
\mathtt{I}
, and because for each
k\geq 0
we get
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k}\otimes Y))\subseteq\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k+1}\otimes Y)
by using the Jacobi identity and that
\mathtt{g}
is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded. Similarly,
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{I})\subseteq\mathtt{I}
since
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{X}\otimes Y)=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{0}\otimes Y)
is one of the summands defining
\mathtt{I}
, and
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k}\otimes Y))\subseteq\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k}\otimes Y)
for all
k\geq 0
, using the Jacobi identity and that each
\mathtt{g}_{k}
is an
\mathtt{X}
-submodule. For the last inclusion we use that
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}\otimes Y)=0
by hypothesis and the Jacobi identity again to check that
\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{X}\otimes Y))=0
and

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k}\otimes Y))

\displaystyle=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{g}_{k})\otimes Y)\subseteq\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k-1}\otimes Y)

\displaystyle\text{for all }k\geq 1.

Hence
\mathtt{I}
is an ideal. As
\mathtt{g}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded operadic Lie algebra, we have that

\displaystyle Y+\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{0}\otimes Y)

\displaystyle=Y+\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{X}\otimes Y)\subseteq\mathtt{g}_{n},

\displaystyle\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{g}_{k}\otimes Y)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathtt{g}_{n+k},

\displaystyle k>0.

Thus
\mathtt{I}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ideal intersecting
\mathtt{g}_{0}=\mathtt{X}
trivially. By maximality of
\mathtt{m}
we get
\mathtt{I}=0
and then also
Y=0
, as desired.
∎

Remark 4.36
.

Consider
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as above and assume that
\rho
is surjective. Let
\mathtt{X}^{\prime}_{\mathtt{d}}\subseteq\mathtt{X}
denote the subalgebra of
\mathtt{X}
generated by the derived subalgebra
\mathtt{X}^{\prime}
and the image of
\mathtt{d}
. Then the derived subalgebra
\mathtt{g}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{g}
coincides with the subalgebra generated by
\mathtt{V}^{*}
,
\mathtt{X}^{\prime}_{\mathtt{d}}
and
\mathtt{V}
, and it satisfies
\mathtt{g}^{\prime}=\mathtt{n}_{-}\oplus\mathtt{X}^{\prime}_{\mathtt{d}}\oplus\mathtt{n}_{+}
.

Remark 4.37
.

Consider
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as above. Assume that
\mathtt{X}=\mathtt{X}_{1}\oplus\mathtt{X}_{2}
as Lie algebras, with
\operatorname{im}\mathtt{d}\subseteq\mathtt{X}_{1}
, and set
\rho_{i}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\rho\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}_{i}}}
for
i=1,2
.
We view
\mathtt{d}
as a map
\mathtt{d}\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{X}_{1}
, so
(\mathtt{X}_{1},\rho_{1},\mathtt{d})
is a reduced contragredient datum.

The
\mathtt{X}_{2}
-actions on
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{V}^{*}
extend to an action
\widehat{\rho_{2}}
on
\operatorname{FLie}(\mathtt{V}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{*})\rtimes_{\rho_{1}}\mathtt{X}_{1}
(with
\mathtt{X}_{2}
acting trivially on
\mathtt{X}_{1}
), which descends to
\mathtt{g}(\mathtt{X}_{1},\rho_{1},\mathtt{d})
. Moreover, it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\mathtt{g}(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})\cong\mathtt{g}(\mathtt{X}_{1},\rho_{1},\mathtt{d})\rtimes_{\widehat{\rho_{2}}}\mathtt{X}_{2}.

Such decomposition will be used in
Section

5
.

One may wonder when a contragredient Lie algebra belongs to
\mathcal{C}
rather than
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{ind}}
. There is a general, easy case where this happens: when the action of
\mathtt{X}
is trivial.

Corollary 4.38
.

Let
(\mathtt{X},\rho,\mathtt{d})
as in
Theorem

4.33
. If
\rho=0
, then
\mathtt{g}=\mathtt{V}^{*}\oplus\mathtt{X}\oplus\mathtt{V}
as
\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects in
\mathcal{C}
and as
\mathtt{X}
-modules in
\mathcal{C}
. The bracket
\mathtt{b}
of
\mathtt{g}
is given by

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{b}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}}

\displaystyle=\mathtt{d},

\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{b}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{b}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{b}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}}}

\displaystyle={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{b}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}}}=0.

Proof.

In
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}=\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
, consider
Y=\mathtt{b}(\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V})=\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{2}
and denote by
\overline{Y}
its image in
\mathtt{g}
. Using the Jacobi identity and that
\rho=0
we check that
\mathtt{b}(\overline{Y}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})=0
in
\mathtt{g}
. Hence
\overline{Y}=0
by
Theorem

4.33

(c)
. In other words
Y\subset\mathtt{m}_{+}
, and as
\mathtt{m}
is an ideal we have that
\mathtt{m}_{+}=\oplus_{n\geq 2}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{n}
since
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{n+1}=\mathtt{b}(\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{n}\otimes\mathtt{V})
. Analogously,
\mathtt{m}_{-}=\oplus_{n\leq 2}\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{n}
, and the statement follows.
∎

Next we show that, as in the classical case, if a quotient of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
admits a symmetric bilinear form with certain non-degeneracy, then it is the minimal quotient
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
.

Proposition 4.39
.

Fix
\mathtt{X}\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
with enough modules and take a reduced datum
(\rho,\mathtt{d})\in\operatorname{RD}(\mathtt{X})
in the sense of
Definition

4.26
.

Let
\mathtt{q}=\oplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathtt{q}_{k}
be a quotient of
\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
by a
\mathbb{Z}
-homogeneous ideal
\mathtt{I}
such that

\mathtt{I}\cap\mathtt{X}=0
.
If
\mathtt{q}
admits a symmetric invariant bilinear form
\mathtt{B}\colon\mathtt{q}\otimes\mathtt{q}\to\mathds{1}
such that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathtt{B}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathtt{q}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{q}_{-k}}}
is non-degenerate for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
then
\mathtt{I}
is the maximal ideal
\mathtt{m}
from
Theorem

4.18

(d)
, i.e.
\mathtt{q}=\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d})
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.28
, we have
\mathtt{I}=\oplus_{k\neq 0,\pm 1}\mathtt{I}_{k}
, where
\mathtt{I}_{k}=\mathtt{I}\cap\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}_{k}
. Suppose that there exists
k>1
such that
\mathtt{I}_{k}\subsetneq\mathtt{m}_{k}
, and take
k
minimal with this condition. Now
Y\coloneq\mathtt{m}_{k}/\mathtt{I}_{k}
is a non-zero subobject of
\mathtt{q}_{k}
such that
\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{q}}(Y\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})=0
; indeed
\mathtt{b}_{\widetilde{\mathtt{g}}}(\mathtt{m}_{k}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})\subseteq\mathtt{m}_{k-1}
, and
\mathtt{m}_{k-1}=\mathtt{I}_{k-1}
by minimality of
k
. Using this fact and that
\mathtt{B}
is invariant, we get

(4.40)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\mathtt{B}\left(\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{q}}(Y\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*})\otimes\mathtt{q}_{1-k}\right)=\mathtt{B}\left(Y\otimes\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{q}}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{q}_{1-k})\right).

On the other hand, by
Remark

4.29
, we have
\mathtt{b}_{\mathtt{q}}(\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{q}_{1-k})=\mathtt{q}_{-k}
. Thus (
4.40
) implies that
0=\mathtt{B}\left(Y\otimes\mathtt{q}_{-k}\right)
, and by assumption on
\mathtt{B}
we get
Y=0
, a contradiction. Hence
\mathtt{I}_{k}=\mathtt{m}_{k}
for all
k>1
. Analogously, one shows that
\mathtt{I}_{k}=\mathtt{m}_{k}
for any
k<-1
. So
\mathtt{I}=\mathtt{m}
, as desired.
∎

4.4.
Symmetrizable data

In this Section we study contragredient data over a Lie algebra
\mathtt{X}
which is assumed to come equipped with a symmetric non-degenerate invariant form. It turns out that for any module
\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V}
one can produce a map
\mathtt{d}\colon\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*}\to\mathtt{X}
satisfying (
4.2
), which happens to be reduced under a further assumption.

Definition 4.41
.

Let
\mathtt{X}\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
.
A
symmetrizable contragredient datum
over
\mathtt{X}
is a pair
(\rho,\mathtt{K})
where

(SD1)

\rho\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\to\mathtt{V}
is an
\mathtt{X}
-module in
\mathcal{C}
such that
\rho(\mathtt{X}\otimes S)\neq 0
for all simple
S\subseteq\mathtt{V}
and
\pi\rho\neq 0
for every projection
\pi\colon\mathtt{V}\to T
onto a simple object
T\subseteq\mathtt{V}
.

(SD2)

\mathtt{K}\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{X}\to\mathds{1}
is a symmetric non-degenerate invariant form.

We denote the set of symmetrizable contragredient data over
\mathtt{X}
by
\operatorname{SD}(\mathtt{X})
.

Given
(\rho,\mathtt{K})\in\operatorname{SD}(\mathtt{X})
, we denote by
\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}}
the image of
\rho
under the isomorphism

(4.42)

\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V},\mathtt{V})\xrightarrow{*}\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{V}^{*}\otimes\mathtt{X}^{*})\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{X}^{*})\cong\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{X}),

where the first map is the dualization functor, the second one is the dualization adjunction and the third one denotes composition with the isomorphism
\mathtt{X}^{*}\cong\mathtt{X}
induced by
\mathtt{K}
.

Example 4.43
.

For
\mathcal{C}=\mathsf{sVec}_{\Bbbk}
, take a classical contragredient datum
(A,\mathbf{p})
as in
(D1)
–
(D4)
, and assume
A
is symmetrizable, i.e.
A=DB
for some invertible
D=\text{diag}(\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{r})
and symmetric
B
. In
Example

3.13
, we extracted from
(A,\mathbf{p})
a contragredient datum
(\rho,d)\in\operatorname{D}(\mathfrak{h})
, where
d\colon V\otimes V^{*}\to\mathfrak{h}
is defined
ad-hoc
by
d(e_{i}\otimes f_{j})=\delta_{ij}h_{i}
and the matrix
A
encodes the action of the
h_{i}
’s. For an alternative, more organic approach, consider the symmetric non-degenerate form
K
on
\mathfrak{h}
such that
K(h_{i},h_{j})=\varepsilon_{i}\varepsilon_{j}b_{ij}
, as defined in
[
20
]
*§2.1. Now it is easy to see that the image of
\rho
under (
4.42
) is given by
\mathtt{d}_{\rho,K}(e_{i}\otimes f_{j})=\delta_{ij}\varepsilon_{i}^{-1}h_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}^{-1}d(e_{i}\otimes f_{j})
, thus
\mathtt{g}(\rho,\mathtt{d}_{\rho,K})\simeq\mathfrak{g}(A,\mathbf{p})
.

We record for later use a compatibility fulfilled by any symmetrizable datum.

Lemma 4.44
.

Given
\mathtt{X}\in\mathsf{Lie}(\mathcal{C})
and
(\rho,\mathtt{K})\in\operatorname{SD}(\mathtt{X})
, the following equality holds:

(4.45)

\displaystyle\mathtt{K}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}}\otimes\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{ev}_{\mathtt{V}}(\rho\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{V}^{*}})\in\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathds{1}).

Moreover,
\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}}
is the unique element in
\mathsf{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathtt{V}\otimes\mathtt{V}^{*},\mathtt{X})
satisfying (
4.45
).

Proof.

Let
\psi\colon\mathtt{X}\to\mathtt{X}^{*}
denote the isomorphism induced by
\mathtt{K}
. Let us proceed diagrammatically; we stick to
4.6
, but since now
\mathtt{d}_{\rho,\mathtt{K}}
is obtained from
\mathtt{K}
and
\rho
, we also need finer vocabulary. Instead of introducing a symbol for
\mathtt{K}\colon\mathtt{X}\otimes\mathtt{X}\to\mathds{1}
, we chose to do so for the morphism
\psi\colon\mathtt{X}\to\mathtt{X}^{*}
it induces. Consider

\operatorname{id}_{\mathtt{X}}=\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 4.44513pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-4.44513pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-17.67181pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox to8.89pt{\vbox to35.34pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.44513pt\lower-34.74364pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -23.62 M 3.54 -23.62 C 3.54 -21.67 1.96 -20.08 0 -20.08 C -1.96 -20.08 -3.54 -21.67 -3.54 -23.62 C -3.54 -25.58 -1.96 -27.17 0 -27.17 C 1.96 -27.17 3.54 -25.58 3.54 -23.62 Z M 0 -23.62}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
